Hey friends, so this is a 3 hour evidence-based studying course that I filmed a few years ago and put on Skillshare, but now I want to make it available to everyone for free. In this class, I'll take you through the well-kept secrets of how to study effectively and organise your workflow to be ready for exams when they come around. We'll also discuss why studying effectively and efficiently is one of the best ways to maintain a work-life balance. 

We'll break down (1) how to understand our content using techniques like the Feynman Method, Active Recall and various note-taking strategies, (2) how to remember our material with Spaced Repetition, Interleaving and various memory techniques, and (3) how to maintain focus while studying, from developing the motivation and discipline to do the work, to taking appropriate breaks and maintaining a healthy work-life balance.

the piano like you can't really learn

the piano just by reading about piano

00:07:29 --> 00:07:33
Theory you have to test yourself by

00:07:31 --> 00:07:35
actually putting it into practice it's

00:07:32 --> 00:07:38
the same when taking any kind of exam

00:07:34 --> 00:07:40
like we all like to think that rereading

00:07:37 --> 00:07:41
information and like sort of try like

00:07:39 --> 00:07:43
it's a case of putting information into

00:07:40 --> 00:07:44
ur brains but actually all of the

00:07:42 --> 00:07:46
vidence shows that actually it's a case

00:07:43 --> 00:07:48
of taking information out of our brains

00:07:45 --> 00:07:49
and the harder we try and work to

00:07:47 --> 00:07:51
retrieve information from our brains

00:07:48 --> 00:07:53
like active retrieval active recall the

00:07:51 --> 00:07:55
harder we try and work for that recall

00:07:52 --> 00:07:56
the more that neural Connection in our

00:07:54 --> 00:07:58
brain is going to be strengthened so to

00:07:55 --> 00:07:59
talk about the research a little bit uh

00:07:57 --> 00:08:01
there is a scientist called Professor

00:07:58 --> 00:08:02
donloski I imagine he's in America

00:08:00 --> 00:08:05
because they're all in America and in

00:08:01 --> 00:08:07
2013 he did he wrote like this really

00:08:04 --> 00:08:09
long paper where he summarized like

00:08:06 --> 00:08:11
thousands of studies humanist colleagues

00:08:08 --> 00:08:12
ummarized thousands of studies done on

00:08:10 --> 00:08:14
college students and school students all

00:08:11 --> 00:08:16
around the world that were all about how

00:08:13 --> 00:08:18
they studied for tests for exams for

00:08:15 --> 00:08:21
whatever and he found that active recall

00:08:17 --> 00:08:22
or testing was very high utility and he

00:08:20 --> 00:08:24
said that on the basis of the evidence

00:08:21 --> 00:08:26
we rate practice testing as having a

00:08:23 --> 00:08:27
high utility testing effects have been

00:08:25 --> 00:08:29
demonstrated across an impressive range

00:08:26 --> 00:08:31
of practice test formats kinds of

00:08:28 --> 00:08:33
material learner ages outcome measures

00:08:30 --> 00:08:35
and retention intervals thus practice

00:08:32 --> 00:08:37
testing has broad applicability

00:08:34 --> 00:08:38
basically a fancy way of saying that we

00:08:36 --> 00:08:40
need to be testing ourselves more and

00:08:37 --> 00:08:41
for me and all of my friends when we

00:08:39 --> 00:08:42
first discovered the power of active

00:08:40 --> 00:08:44
recall in our second year of med school

00:08:42 --> 00:08:46
when we had the psychology lecture it

00:08:43 --> 00:08:48
completely changed our lives because we

00:08:45 --> 00:08:49
re like oh my God this is incredible

00:08:47 --> 00:08:51
all I have to do is like start testing

00:08:48 --> 00:08:53
myself a little bit more rather than

00:08:50 --> 00:08:54
just try and reread and highlight and

00:08:52 --> 00:08:56
all that you know useless stuff that we

00:08:53 --> 00:08:58
re doing in the past

00:08:55 --> 00:08:59
so we've talked about active recall and

00:08:57 --> 00:09:01
we've talked about how it's the single

00:08:58 --> 00:09:03
best way to make us remember anything

00:09:00 --> 00:09:05
but a question that students often ask

00:09:02 --> 00:09:06
is okay fine active recall like testing

00:09:04 --> 00:09:09
myself is all well and good and I'm

00:09:06 --> 00:09:11
going to test myself more but surely I

00:09:08 --> 00:09:12
need to learn the content first before I

00:09:10 --> 00:09:14
can test myself on it and that's a very

00:09:11 --> 00:09:15
reasonable you know claim to make and

00:09:13 --> 00:09:17
what's been really helpful for me and

00:09:14 --> 00:09:19
most of my friends is that we start

00:09:16 --> 00:09:21
hinking of testing as just a part of

00:09:18 --> 00:09:22
the learning process itself for example

00:09:20 --> 00:09:24
like reading a textbook you know and

00:09:21 --> 00:09:26
preparing for human physiology these

00:09:23 --> 00:09:29
days at the end of every paragraph or

00:09:25 --> 00:09:30
every other paragraph I will stop I will

00:09:28 --> 00:09:32
ike sort of metaphorically close the

00:09:29 --> 00:09:34
book and or look away and I'll ask

00:09:31 --> 00:09:36
myself okay what have I just read what

00:09:33 --> 00:09:37
are the key Concepts from what I've just

00:09:35 --> 00:09:39
read and can I phrase this in my own

00:09:36 --> 00:09:41
words and I'm doing that with the book

00:09:38 --> 00:09:42
closed I'm not wasting time by writing

00:09:40 --> 00:09:43
out a summary of stuff with the book

00:09:41 --> 00:09:45
open because that's completely passive

00:09:42 --> 00:09:46
and completely pointless what I'm doing

00:09:44 --> 00:09:48
is I'm closing the book and then I'm

00:09:45 --> 00:09:49
trying to actively recall what I've just

00:09:47 --> 00:09:51
read and the fact that I'm doing it

00:09:48 --> 00:09:52
here and then also helps with the

00:09:50 --> 00:09:54
forgetting curve that again we've talked

00:09:51 --> 00:09:55
about a bit and we'll talk about a bit

00:09:53 --> 00:09:57
later because as soon as I've read that

00:09:54 --> 00:09:59
paragraph off like reading that

00:09:56 --> 00:10:00
paragraph is a passive process and

00:09:58 --> 00:10:01
therefore my brain doesn't have to work

00:09:59 --> 00:10:04
very hard at reading the paragraph our

00:10:00 --> 00:10:05
brain is I it's not a Perfect Analogy

00:10:03 --> 00:10:07
but it's sort of like a muscle in that

00:10:04 --> 00:10:08
he harder it has to work to do

00:10:06 --> 00:10:10
something the more likely it is to be

00:10:07 --> 00:10:11
able to do that thing better in the

00:10:09 --> 00:10:13
future testing ourselves is really

00:10:10 --> 00:10:14
important and honestly if there's just

00:10:12 --> 00:10:16
one thing you take away from this entire

00:10:13 --> 00:10:17
class I don't know how long this class

00:10:15 --> 00:10:18
is going to be but if there's only one

00:10:16 --> 00:10:20
thing you take away from it please let

00:10:17 --> 00:10:22
it be that we should all be testing

00:10:19 --> 00:10:24
ourselves probably two to three times as

00:10:21 --> 00:10:25
much as we currently are but that

00:10:23 --> 00:10:27
doesn't involve just testing ourselves

00:10:24 --> 00:10:28
as a sort of judgment exercise once

00:10:26 --> 00:10:30
we've learned a topic it involves

00:10:27 --> 00:10:32
testing ourselves throughout because

00:10:29 --> 00:10:33
again the more a brain has to work to do

00:10:31 --> 00:10:35
something the more effortful the

00:10:33 --> 00:10:37
learning is the more the connections are

00:10:34 --> 00:10:39
likely to be strengthened taking notes

00:10:36 --> 00:10:41
is a little bit controversial because in

00:10:38 --> 00:10:44
general if you look at the evidence for

00:10:40 --> 00:10:46
it summarizing I.E kind of writing

00:10:43 --> 00:10:48
summaries of stuff with the book open is

00:10:45 --> 00:10:50
generally considered a fairly low like

00:10:47 --> 00:10:51
low utility thing according to professor

00:10:49 --> 00:10:52
danloskey and his colleagues the guys

00:10:50 --> 00:10:54
who reviewed thousands of studies that

00:10:51 --> 00:10:56
looked at this sort of stuff they found

00:10:53 --> 00:10:58
that the people who sort of some

00:10:55 --> 00:10:59
morized content and don't do very well

00:10:57 --> 00:11:01
compared to the people that just test

00:10:58 --> 00:11:02
hemselves on the content because

00:11:00 --> 00:11:04
summarizing content is usually a passive

00:11:01 --> 00:11:06
process and going back to a fundamental

00:11:03 --> 00:11:07
idea the harder we have to the harder

00:11:05 --> 00:11:09
our brains have to work to do something

00:11:06 --> 00:11:11
the more likely we are to remember the

00:11:08 --> 00:11:12
thing that's partly why taking notes are

00:11:10 --> 00:11:14
controversial firstly I think kind of

00:11:12 --> 00:11:15
splitting it up into these two like

00:11:13 --> 00:11:17
thinking about it in these two different

00:11:14 --> 00:11:19
ways is how I think about it so during

00:11:16 --> 00:11:20
class and then after class so in this

00:11:18 --> 00:11:22
video we're talking about during class

00:11:19 --> 00:11:23
the first thing to say is that there's

00:11:21 --> 00:11:25
quite a lot of evidence about this sort

00:11:22 --> 00:11:27
of stuff but basically handwriting on

00:11:24 --> 00:11:29
otes is better for us than typing up

00:11:26 --> 00:11:30
our notes and there's lots of evidence

00:11:28 --> 00:11:32
about this but again this might seem

00:11:29 --> 00:11:33
Blasphemous to those of us who take our

00:11:31 --> 00:11:34
laptops and see everyone with their

00:11:32 --> 00:11:35
MacBook airs out and like you know

00:11:33 --> 00:11:37
bashing stuff for an Evernote or notion

00:11:34 --> 00:11:38
or OneNote or whatever the you know

00:11:36 --> 00:11:40
note-taking app of the flavor of the

00:11:37 --> 00:11:42
month is but handwriting notes does have

00:11:39 --> 00:11:44
more evidence behind it so firstly uh

00:11:41 --> 00:11:45
andwriting notes is supposedly better

00:11:43 --> 00:11:47
because it helps us think a little bit

00:11:44 --> 00:11:49
more intensely for example there was a

00:11:46 --> 00:11:50
study done by Washington University in

00:11:48 --> 00:11:52
St Louis in America and they were

00:11:49 --> 00:11:53
comparing students who took notes by

00:11:51 --> 00:11:55
hand versus students who took notes with

00:11:52 --> 00:11:57
a laptop during a lecture and they found

00:11:54 --> 00:11:58
that if they were tested immediately

00:11:56 --> 00:11:59
after the lecture the ones with the

00:11:57 --> 00:12:01
laptop did a little bit better than the

00:11:58 --> 00:12:03
ones who were taking notes by hand but

00:12:00 --> 00:12:04
if they were tested 24 hours after the

00:12:02 --> 00:12:05
lecture then they found that the ones

00:12:03 --> 00:12:07
who did the handwriting performed better

00:12:04 --> 00:12:08
than the ones who typed up stuff with

00:12:06 --> 00:12:10
e laptop and when they repeated the

00:12:07 --> 00:12:11
test a week later they found that the

00:12:09 --> 00:12:13
handwriting students again you know had

00:12:10 --> 00:12:14
a better grasp of the concepts than the

00:12:12 --> 00:12:16
ones who took notes just using a laptop

00:12:13 --> 00:12:18
but to be honest the whole taking notes

00:12:15 --> 00:12:19
in class for me thing like ever since I

00:12:17 --> 00:12:22
realized that taking notes is not very

00:12:18 --> 00:12:23
effective the only reason realistically

00:12:21 --> 00:12:26
that I actually take notes in class is

00:12:22 --> 00:12:27
because it helps keep me awake like it's

00:12:25 --> 00:12:28
o hard as you will know if you've been

00:12:26 --> 00:12:30
to University lectures it is literally

00:12:27 --> 00:12:33
so hard to stay awake during a lecture

00:12:29 --> 00:12:34
and so I found that for me that you know

00:12:32 --> 00:12:36
if I if I sit in the front row and I

00:12:33 --> 00:12:38
force myself to take notes it means I'm

00:12:35 --> 00:12:40
far more likely to stay awake and then

00:12:37 --> 00:12:42
the notes that I've got are usually are

00:12:39 --> 00:12:43
fairly minimalistic mostly based on the

00:12:41 --> 00:12:45
subheadings rather than the kind of

00:12:42 --> 00:12:48
nitty-gritty detail of the content which

00:12:44 --> 00:12:49
means I do get a broad brush stroke

00:12:47 --> 00:12:51
understanding of the content even if I

00:12:48 --> 00:12:52
don't get all the details there and then

00:12:50 --> 00:12:54
but most importantly it keeps me awake

00:12:51 --> 00:12:57
so that's just a little bit of advice

00:12:53 --> 00:12:59
about how to take notes during us

00:12:56 --> 00:13:01
Okay so we've talked about taking notes

00:12:58 --> 00:13:03
during class let's now talk about taking

00:13:00 --> 00:13:05
notes after class and I said for me the

00:13:02 --> 00:13:07
main reason to take notes during class

00:13:04 --> 00:13:09
is to keep myself awake but for me the

00:13:06 --> 00:13:11
main reason to take notes after class is

00:13:08 --> 00:13:13
a to build my active recall questions

00:13:10 --> 00:13:15
and B to consolidate my understanding of

00:13:12 --> 00:13:16
the subject which is why this is in the

00:13:14 --> 00:13:18
understand section because we're still

00:13:15 --> 00:13:20
trying to make sure that we understand

00:13:17 --> 00:13:21
all of our content before we worry about

00:13:19 --> 00:13:23
rying to remember trying to memorize it

00:13:20 --> 00:13:25
so taking notes outside of class I think

00:13:22 --> 00:13:27
basically two main two main reasons for

00:13:24 --> 00:13:29
it number one is to be able to use

00:13:26 --> 00:13:31
multiple sources to help understand

00:13:28 --> 00:13:33
stuff so often if we're in a university

00:13:30 --> 00:13:35
lecture it can be kind of hard to keep

00:13:32 --> 00:13:38
track of what the lecturer is saying and

00:13:34 --> 00:13:39
take down absolutely every detail and it

00:13:37 --> 00:13:41
can be quite tricky to understand the

00:13:38 --> 00:13:43
stuff as it's going along which means we

00:13:40 --> 00:13:44
probably need to look over our notes

00:13:42 --> 00:13:46
again and we might need to add more

00:13:43 --> 00:13:48
little bits and going back to the

00:13:45 --> 00:13:49
Feynman technique you know the ultimate

00:13:47 --> 00:13:51
objective is to be able to explain to a

00:13:48 --> 00:13:54
five-year-old or to be able to explain

00:13:50 --> 00:13:56
to a friend exactly how this concept

00:13:53 --> 00:13:57
works and so if we ever get to a point

00:13:55 --> 00:13:59
where we think okay if that makes sense

00:13:56 --> 00:14:00
then make sense that makes sense but and

00:13:58 --> 00:14:02
you know I don't I don't get how we got

00:13:59 --> 00:14:04
from there to there or you know that bit

00:14:01 --> 00:14:05
doesn't quite make sense at that point

00:14:03 --> 00:14:07
we're going to need to use different

00:14:04 --> 00:14:09
sources so Google Wikipedia textbook

00:14:06 --> 00:14:11
lecture notes whatever and then we can

00:14:08 --> 00:14:13
consolidate our notes so that when we're

00:14:10 --> 00:14:15
looking through it again we sort of get

00:14:12 --> 00:14:17
his more coherent narrative being built

00:14:14 --> 00:14:18
up these days I use the app notion for

00:14:16 --> 00:14:20
that so for example if there's something

00:14:17 --> 00:14:23
that I don't understand then I will

00:14:19 --> 00:14:24
create a separate page in notion and

00:14:22 --> 00:14:27
sort of add information usually from

00:14:23 --> 00:14:28
Google and Wikipedia and occasionally if

00:14:26 --> 00:14:30
I have to I'll go into a textbook and

00:14:27 --> 00:14:32
I'll screenshot something using the

00:14:29 --> 00:14:34
screenshot tool shove it into notion and

00:14:31 --> 00:14:36
so I've got that information if if I

00:14:33 --> 00:14:37
need it loads of my friends including me

00:14:35 --> 00:14:39
when we were on our first year of med

00:14:36 --> 00:14:41
school we were like okay at the end of

00:14:38 --> 00:14:42
every lecture I need to make notes on

00:14:40 --> 00:14:45
the lecture and then I need to revise

00:14:41 --> 00:14:47
from my notes and the people who did

00:14:44 --> 00:14:49
this unless they were working like you

00:14:46 --> 00:14:51
know many many hours a day it became

00:14:48 --> 00:14:53
unsustainable because most of the stuff

00:14:50 --> 00:14:55
that we were tested on was coming

00:14:52 --> 00:14:57
directly from our lecture notes and the

00:14:54 --> 00:14:59
xtra stuff that these people were

00:14:56 --> 00:15:00
adding and then you know I say I say

00:14:58 --> 00:15:02
these people like I was doing it myself

00:14:59 --> 00:15:04
the extra stuff that we were adding

00:15:01 --> 00:15:06
wasn't that useful it was helpful for

00:15:03 --> 00:15:08
our understanding but I think it was

00:15:05 --> 00:15:10
this fundamental misunderstanding that

00:15:07 --> 00:15:12
you know we have to take notes from the

00:15:09 --> 00:15:13
lecture and we absolutely do not have to

00:15:11 --> 00:15:15
take notes on the lecture for it to work

00:15:12 --> 00:15:17
and I remember in like the Easter

00:15:14 --> 00:15:19
holidays before our exams I would have

00:15:16 --> 00:15:21
friends being like Oh my god I've still

00:15:18 --> 00:15:23
got 84 lectures to take notes on and

00:15:20 --> 00:15:24
only then can I start my revision and

00:15:22 --> 00:15:27
that always seemed to sit like not very

00:15:24 --> 00:15:28
well with me because even then I sort of

00:15:26 --> 00:15:30
thought that this whole taking notes

00:15:27 --> 00:15:32
from lectures thing is possibly a little

00:15:29 --> 00:15:33
bit pointless but anyway going back to

00:15:31 --> 00:15:35
the main point I said the point number

00:15:32 --> 00:15:37
one was that taking notes after class is

00:15:34 --> 00:15:39
emi-useful in in that it helps build up

00:15:36 --> 00:15:40
the picture to consolidate our

00:15:38 --> 00:15:42
understanding of it and the second

00:15:39 --> 00:15:44
reason why taking notes after class is

00:15:41 --> 00:15:46
helpful is because similar to in the

00:15:43 --> 00:15:47
previous video we can use the Cornell

00:15:45 --> 00:15:50
note-taking system to write questions

00:15:46 --> 00:15:51
for ourselves and so for example if

00:15:49 --> 00:15:53
we've taken notes in class then we can

00:15:51 --> 00:15:54
look at those notes after class and

00:15:52 --> 00:15:56
think okay what are the different

00:15:53 --> 00:15:59
questions that I can I can make from

00:15:55 --> 00:16:01
this so let's say we've taken notes on I

00:15:58 --> 00:16:02
don't know how aquaporins work in cell

00:16:00 --> 00:16:04
membranes or the structure of cell

00:16:01 --> 00:16:06
membrane biosynthesis or the discovery

00:16:03 --> 00:16:07
of cell membranes or whatever I'm

00:16:05 --> 00:16:09
thinking about cell membranes because I

00:16:06 --> 00:16:11
recently did a notion video about that

00:16:08 --> 00:16:13
and that was kind of top of my mind the

00:16:10 --> 00:16:15
recency effect in Psychology it's called

00:16:12 --> 00:16:17
anyway let's say we've got notes and all

00:16:14 --> 00:16:19
that stuff like the notes themselves are

00:16:16 --> 00:16:20
pointless because we don't want to just

00:16:18 --> 00:16:22
be rereading the notes what we want to

00:16:19 --> 00:16:24
do is we want to create questions for

00:16:21 --> 00:16:26
ourselves based on the notes so again

00:16:23 --> 00:16:28
what I do is I use notion and I use the

00:16:25 --> 00:16:29
toggle feature of Notions to write a

00:16:27 --> 00:16:31
question for myself so one of the

00:16:28 --> 00:16:33
questions might be what is the function

00:16:30 --> 00:16:34
of the cell membrane and within that I

00:16:32 --> 00:16:37
would write my notes on the cell

00:16:33 --> 00:16:38
membrane but I would hide them so the

00:16:36 --> 00:16:40
only way I can see the notes on the cell

00:16:37 --> 00:16:42
membrane is if I've already looked at

00:16:39 --> 00:16:43
he question first and then I make it a

00:16:41 --> 00:16:45
point that whenever I see a question in

00:16:42 --> 00:16:47
my notes I actually think about how will

00:16:44 --> 00:16:49
I answer this question which is active

00:16:46 --> 00:16:50
recall in action sort of the Feynman

00:16:48 --> 00:16:52
technique in action if it's a concept

00:16:49 --> 00:16:54
hat I can explain to someone else

00:16:51 --> 00:16:55
but it ensures that I'm not just

00:16:53 --> 00:16:57
passively rereading the notes I'm

00:16:54 --> 00:16:59
actually actively dredging information

00:16:56 --> 00:17:00
up from my brain which coming back to

00:16:58 --> 00:17:02
ur main point the thread that runs

00:16:59 --> 00:17:04
through this entire class is that the

00:17:01 --> 00:17:05
more effort for learning is the more

00:17:03 --> 00:17:08
likely we are to make the information

00:17:04 --> 00:17:10
stick and so yeah going back uh to the

00:17:07 --> 00:17:12
topic of this video taking notes after

00:17:09 --> 00:17:14
class I think is a matter of expanding

00:17:11 --> 00:17:16
uh using multiple sources to improve our

00:17:13 --> 00:17:17
understanding and secondly making sure

00:17:15 --> 00:17:19
that we've written active recall

00:17:16 --> 00:17:22
questions for ourselves in one way or

00:17:18 --> 00:17:24
another to help actually consolidate the

00:17:21 --> 00:17:25
information to help use active recall to

00:17:23 --> 00:17:27
retrieve it from our brains and to help

00:17:24 --> 00:17:28
that information stick and at that point

00:17:26 --> 00:17:29
when we're going through our notes we

00:17:27 --> 00:17:31
could even if we wanted to we could turn

00:17:28 --> 00:17:32
it into flashcards if we're using a

00:17:30 --> 00:17:34
flashcard app we'll talk a lot more

00:17:31 --> 00:17:36
about flashcards in the remember section

00:17:33 --> 00:17:37
because there's a few pitfalls people

00:17:35 --> 00:17:39
can run into with flashcards but yeah

00:17:36 --> 00:17:41
that was taking notes after class I hope

00:17:38 --> 00:17:43
you found that very helpful Point number

00:17:40 --> 00:17:44
one expand the sources to include

00:17:42 --> 00:17:46
multiple sources and understand the

00:17:43 --> 00:17:47
topic better Point number two please

00:17:45 --> 00:17:48
let's write questions for ourselves so

00:17:46 --> 00:17:50
that we can actually use active recall

00:17:48 --> 00:17:51
rather than passive rereading so thanks

00:17:49 --> 00:17:52
for watching and I'll see you in the

00:17:50 --> 00:17:54
next video

00:17:51 --> 00:17:56
kay so let's now talk about this thing

00:17:53 --> 00:17:57
called scoping the subject and I think

00:17:55 --> 00:17:59
this is one of the most important

00:17:56 --> 00:18:00
mantras I first learned this from a

00:17:58 --> 00:18:01
friend of mine in med school who used to

00:17:59 --> 00:18:03
do really well in exams I'm sure he

00:18:00 --> 00:18:05
still does if he takes exams to this day

00:18:02 --> 00:18:07
um but he coined the phrase scoping the

00:18:04 --> 00:18:10
subject and basically the idea is that

00:18:06 --> 00:18:12
in in order to understand something we

00:18:09 --> 00:18:14
need to understand where I understand we

00:18:11 --> 00:18:16
need to understand where it fits in Into

00:18:13 --> 00:18:17
The Wider picture and there's a phrase

00:18:15 --> 00:18:19
that I really like that I use a lot in

00:18:16 --> 00:18:21
this course which is we don't want to

00:18:18 --> 00:18:24
miss the forest from the trees like it's

00:18:20 --> 00:18:25
it can be so easy when we're sort of in

00:18:23 --> 00:18:27
the day-to-day studying of school

00:18:24 --> 00:18:29
University whatever it can be so easy to

00:18:26 --> 00:18:31
focus on the detail to focus on okay

00:18:28 --> 00:18:32
I've got to get through lecture seven of

00:18:30 --> 00:18:36
pathology and lecture nine of physiology

00:18:31 --> 00:18:37
now that we if we like if we don't take

00:18:35 --> 00:18:39
the time to do it it can be so easy to

00:18:36 --> 00:18:40
just forget what the bigger picture is

00:18:38 --> 00:18:42
and I really used to have this problem

00:18:39 --> 00:18:43
so when I first started out in my fourth

00:18:41 --> 00:18:45
year of med school we started out in

00:18:42 --> 00:18:46
clinical medicine and I had this idea

00:18:44 --> 00:18:48
that I was going to memorize the Oxford

00:18:45 --> 00:18:49
Handbook of clinical medicine which is

00:18:47 --> 00:18:52
one of the you know core texts that we

00:18:48 --> 00:18:52
had to use and I for some reason I had

00:18:51 --> 00:18:54
this terrible idea that I was gonna

00:18:51 --> 00:18:56
fflict of random pages and if I did

00:18:53 --> 00:18:57
that enough then over time I'd memorize

00:18:55 --> 00:18:59
the whole thing like clearly this is

00:18:57 --> 00:19:01
completely like terrible because there

00:18:58 --> 00:19:03
was no understanding there it was pure

00:19:00 --> 00:19:06
memorization and you know there was no

00:19:02 --> 00:19:08
way for me to hang details on the

00:19:05 --> 00:19:10
skeleton and that's sort of how I think

00:19:07 --> 00:19:11
about it today like either hanging

00:19:09 --> 00:19:13
details on a skeleton of the outline or

00:19:10 --> 00:19:14
thinking about it as like a tree

00:19:12 --> 00:19:15
understanding a tree and then

00:19:13 --> 00:19:17
understanding the branches and then

00:19:14 --> 00:19:18
understanding the leaves so we don't

00:19:16 --> 00:19:20
want to be focusing on the leaves or the

00:19:17 --> 00:19:21
branches we want to be looking at the

00:19:19 --> 00:19:23
tree and thinking about where the

00:19:20 --> 00:19:24
branches are going off of the tree and

00:19:22 --> 00:19:26
so that's part of what scoping the

00:19:23 --> 00:19:28
subject is it's about kind of taking a

00:19:25 --> 00:19:30
few hours to literally go through every

00:19:27 --> 00:19:32
single topic within our subject and try

00:19:29 --> 00:19:34
and sub categorize the different topics

00:19:31 --> 00:19:35
the reason I'm going on about this so

00:19:33 --> 00:19:37
much is because this is something that I

00:19:34 --> 00:19:39
used to not do when I was in secondary

00:19:36 --> 00:19:40
school and early on in University but

00:19:38 --> 00:19:43
hen since I started doing it I found

00:19:39 --> 00:19:45
that now when I'm learning details I can

00:19:42 --> 00:19:46
appreciate the bigger picture I can

00:19:44 --> 00:19:48
appreciate the bird's eye view and

00:19:45 --> 00:19:50
there's an analogy I like I like in in

00:19:47 --> 00:19:51
medicine which I'm sure applies to other

00:19:49 --> 00:19:52
subjects as well which is that you've

00:19:50 --> 00:19:54
got the bird's eye view and then you

00:19:51 --> 00:19:56
've got the Frog's eye view and the

00:19:53 --> 00:19:58
annoying thing is that most of the exams

00:19:55 --> 00:20:00
test us on the Frog's eye view like you

00:19:57 --> 00:20:02
know that nitty-gritty minutiae detail

00:19:59 --> 00:20:04
but actually the bird's eye view is the

00:20:01 --> 00:20:05
thing that's important for a helping us

00:20:03 --> 00:20:07
understand the subject and be at least

00:20:04 --> 00:20:09
in medicine for helping us actually

00:20:06 --> 00:20:11
apply it in real life because really in

00:20:08 --> 00:20:13
real life as a doctor we don't like we

00:20:10 --> 00:20:15
never really think about the intricacies

00:20:12 --> 00:20:16
of how the receptors work whereas that

00:20:14 --> 00:20:17
was what we were tested on in medical

00:20:15 --> 00:20:19
school we just think about the broad

00:20:16 --> 00:20:20
picture so starting off with the broad

00:20:18 --> 00:20:22
picture and then narrowing down is I

00:20:19 --> 00:20:23
think a much better way than you know

00:20:21 --> 00:20:24
that mistake that we all make of

00:20:22 --> 00:20:26
starting with the details and then

00:20:23 --> 00:20:27
forgetting the forest from the trees so

00:20:25 --> 00:20:28
that's what I'll say about scoping the

00:20:26 --> 00:20:31
subject

00:20:27 --> 00:20:32
hey guys this is a bonus segment within

00:20:30 --> 00:20:34
the class and it's all about the

00:20:31 --> 00:20:36
importance of understanding this isn't

00:20:33 --> 00:20:37
really like cool material so feel free

00:20:35 --> 00:20:39
to skip ahead to the next video if

00:20:36 --> 00:20:40
you're really rushed for time but this

00:20:38 --> 00:20:42
a conversation that I had with my

00:20:39 --> 00:20:43
friend assade he is currently a final

00:20:41 --> 00:20:45
year medical student at Cambridge

00:20:42 --> 00:20:47
University he consistently ranked like

00:20:44 --> 00:20:49
in the top three in the year group

00:20:46 --> 00:20:50
um for the last several years so he's

00:20:48 --> 00:20:51
really really good at taking exams and

00:20:49 --> 00:20:53
obviously because he's a Cambridge he

00:20:50 --> 00:20:54
absolutely smashed his gccs and his

00:20:52 --> 00:20:56
a-levels and he's been using this

00:20:53 --> 00:20:57
knowledge of effective studying to

00:20:55 --> 00:20:58
really absolutely smash these exams ever

00:20:56 --> 00:20:59
since he found out about these

00:20:57 --> 00:21:00
techniques so in this video we're

00:20:58 --> 00:21:02
talking about the importance of

00:21:00 --> 00:21:04
understanding and I start by asking him

00:21:01 --> 00:21:06
what it actually means to understand

00:21:03 --> 00:21:09
something when you understand something

00:21:05 --> 00:21:11
it's a lot easier to retain and I I

00:21:08 --> 00:21:14
think more generally and

00:21:10 --> 00:21:16
I think it's part of how I study I

00:21:13 --> 00:21:18
really struggle to bring myself to

00:21:15 --> 00:21:21
Simply memorized details without having

00:21:18 --> 00:21:23
any kind of understanding not because I

00:21:20 --> 00:21:26
can't do it and in fact to a certain

00:21:22 --> 00:21:30
degree in second year I did do that to

00:21:25 --> 00:21:33
some extent but it just doesn't feel

00:21:29 --> 00:21:36
right to me and I I have this kind of

00:21:32 --> 00:21:38
nagging feeling inside of me to really

00:21:35 --> 00:21:39
understand what's going on and how do

00:21:37 --> 00:21:42
you define understand like how do you

00:21:38 --> 00:21:42
know you've understood something

00:21:41 --> 00:21:47
I suppose for me it's about coherence

00:21:44 --> 00:21:48
within my mind

00:21:46 --> 00:21:51
um

00:21:47 --> 00:21:53
if there aren't any kind of unexplained

00:21:50 --> 00:21:56
questions that I have left on the topic

00:21:52 --> 00:21:58
then I'll consider it understood if I

00:21:55 --> 00:22:00
can kind of derive the topic through

00:21:57 --> 00:22:03
some basic first principles within

00:21:59 --> 00:22:04
my mind I I feel as if I

00:22:02 --> 00:22:05
understood it

00:22:03 --> 00:22:06
um and I guess that's different to other

00:22:04 --> 00:22:08
people who feel like they've understood

00:22:06 --> 00:22:11
it when they can say explain it that

00:22:07 --> 00:22:12
wasn't necessarily

00:22:10 --> 00:22:16
um what I used it was more about

00:22:11 --> 00:22:17
internal coherence okay but what's the

00:22:15 --> 00:22:19
difference between internal coherence

00:22:16 --> 00:22:21
and being able to to explain it and I

00:22:18 --> 00:22:23
also because often my proxy for do I

00:22:20 --> 00:22:24
understand something is would I feasibly

00:22:22 --> 00:22:26
be able to explain this to a

00:22:23 --> 00:22:28
five-year-old and I feel like for me

00:22:25 --> 00:22:30
when I have internal coherence it means

00:22:27 --> 00:22:32
I can be like okay well you know if you

00:22:29 --> 00:22:33
lose 20 of your blood volume then

00:22:31 --> 00:22:35
that happens and therefore that happens

00:22:32 --> 00:22:38
and that happens and it's a chain of

00:22:34 --> 00:22:39
events in my mind so absolutely I mean I

00:22:37 --> 00:22:41
think sort of the gold standard is being

00:22:38 --> 00:22:43
able to explain it to a five-year-old

00:22:40 --> 00:22:47
that that really indicates like

00:22:42 --> 00:22:50
quite in-depth conceptual Clarity

00:22:46 --> 00:22:51
um however you can't always necessarily

00:22:49 --> 00:22:52
have that

00:22:50 --> 00:22:54
um

00:22:51 --> 00:22:56
I think especially in physiology and

00:22:53 --> 00:22:58
neurophysiology there are some quite

00:22:55 --> 00:23:01
complex

00:22:57 --> 00:23:03
um Concepts that you need to understand

00:23:02 --> 00:23:06
for quite a few of them there'll be

00:23:04 --> 00:23:08
times where I'll understand them for

00:23:05 --> 00:23:12
seconds at a time and then lose

00:23:07 --> 00:23:14
understanding and and I know the method

00:23:11 --> 00:23:16
I need to re-understand them I know the

00:23:13 --> 00:23:17
diagram that will make me understand

00:23:15 --> 00:23:20
them again

00:23:16 --> 00:23:23
but usually because the comp because the

00:23:19 --> 00:23:24
concepts are quite complex

00:23:22 --> 00:23:26
sometimes I'll only be understanding

00:23:23 --> 00:23:29
them whilst contemplating the diagram

00:23:25 --> 00:23:31
either on paper or in my mind okay and

00:23:28 --> 00:23:33
then at that and then but once you've

00:23:30 --> 00:23:35
understood that you're you're then happy

00:23:32 --> 00:23:37
to just remember it for for the exam

00:23:34 --> 00:23:38
because you know that at one point you

00:23:36 --> 00:23:41
had that Moment of clarity where where

00:23:37 --> 00:23:42
it all made sense so I mean even then I

00:23:40 --> 00:23:44
wouldn't necessarily just call it

00:23:41 --> 00:23:47
remembering a random fact or anything

00:23:43 --> 00:23:50
like that without meaning

00:23:46 --> 00:23:55
I'll remember how I came to understand

00:23:49 --> 00:23:57
it and I'll usually if I need to be able

00:23:54 --> 00:24:00
to work out how to understand it again

00:23:56 --> 00:24:02
and I guess that's this is probably a

00:23:59 --> 00:24:04
bit more relevant in neurophysiology

00:24:01 --> 00:24:07
than say physiology because it has

00:24:03 --> 00:24:09
perhaps the most kind of advanced

00:24:06 --> 00:24:11
concepts within medicine some of the

00:24:08 --> 00:24:13
most abstract Concepts which really seem

00:24:10 --> 00:24:16
very divorced from

00:24:12 --> 00:24:17
things you'll find in everyday life

00:24:15 --> 00:24:19
[Music]

00:24:16 --> 00:24:21
such that unless you're unless you've

00:24:18 --> 00:24:24
got a mental image built up where you're

00:24:20 --> 00:24:27
Imagining the circuits all at once it's

00:24:23 --> 00:24:29
quite difficult to really remember

00:24:26 --> 00:24:32
what's going on but what you what you

00:24:28 --> 00:24:34
can remember is a basic outline which

00:24:31 --> 00:24:37
you can reconstruct in your mind and

00:24:33 --> 00:24:38
somewhat run as a model understanding a

00:24:36 --> 00:24:40
subject is it is like having a mental

00:24:37 --> 00:24:42
model of that subject in your mind

00:24:39 --> 00:24:43
absolutely yeah and to some extent you

00:24:41 --> 00:24:45
can explain bit to those to a

00:24:42 --> 00:24:47
five-year-old but to some extent as as

00:24:44 --> 00:24:49
you're saying it's just there and it

00:24:46 --> 00:24:51
would you probably could if you had to

00:24:48 --> 00:24:53
put all the time and effort to figure

00:24:50 --> 00:24:55
out how to simplify it down to explain

00:24:52 --> 00:24:56
to a five-year-old but it's

00:24:54 --> 00:24:59
unsustainable to do that for everything

00:24:55 --> 00:25:00
I mean I guess the one example I could

00:24:58 --> 00:25:03
give

00:24:59 --> 00:25:06
um so I'm currently doing supervisions

00:25:02 --> 00:25:08
in neurophysiology for

00:25:05 --> 00:25:09
um the second year students and the next

00:25:07 --> 00:25:12
supervision that I'm planning to give is

00:25:08 --> 00:25:15
on memory uh specifically on the role of

00:25:11 --> 00:25:17
the hippocampus and

00:25:14 --> 00:25:18
as far as it's taught in the course

00:25:16 --> 00:25:20
veryone knows that the hippocampus is

00:25:17 --> 00:25:22
involved in memory if you have damage to

00:25:19 --> 00:25:24
the hippocampus it interferes with your

00:25:21 --> 00:25:26
ability to perform new memories and

00:25:23 --> 00:25:28
sometimes you lose old memories as well

00:25:25 --> 00:25:32
but the reason for that isn't quite

00:25:27 --> 00:25:34
clear and I remember learning and

00:25:31 --> 00:25:36
understanding back in second year that

00:25:33 --> 00:25:37
he way the hippocampus works is that it

00:25:35 --> 00:25:39
works as an index it doesn't store

00:25:36 --> 00:25:41
memories but it's just like an index in

00:25:38 --> 00:25:43
a book documents where in the cortex the

00:25:40 --> 00:25:45
memories are stored so if you ever want

00:25:42 --> 00:25:46
o re-summon a memory it can recruit all

00:25:44 --> 00:25:47
the parts of the cortex that are needed

00:25:45 --> 00:25:51
at the same time

00:25:46 --> 00:25:52
now there's a very specific circuit

00:25:50 --> 00:25:55
structure which allows that to take

00:25:51 --> 00:25:59
place within the hippocampus I don't

00:25:54 --> 00:26:01
remember that at the moment and

00:25:58 --> 00:26:03
it would take a while for me probably

00:26:00 --> 00:26:06
half an hour to really work through the

00:26:02 --> 00:26:08
diagram and re-understand it but because

00:26:05 --> 00:26:11
I know that basic concept that the

00:26:07 --> 00:26:12
hippocampus is working like an index and

00:26:10 --> 00:26:14
that there are these basic neurological

00:26:11 --> 00:26:16
principles like neurons that fire

00:26:13 --> 00:26:19
together why together

00:26:15 --> 00:26:21
I can I can basically use those guiding

00:26:18 --> 00:26:23
principles to re-understand what I

00:26:20 --> 00:26:25
understood in in second year

00:26:22 --> 00:26:27
hello so this is another bonus segment

00:26:24 --> 00:26:30
and in this video uh me and assade are

00:26:26 --> 00:26:32
discussing the idea of developing a

00:26:29 --> 00:26:33
syllabus for your subject and this is

00:26:31 --> 00:26:35
particularly important in subjects where

00:26:32 --> 00:26:36
you're kind of more rely on

00:26:34 --> 00:26:37
understanding than you are in

00:26:35 --> 00:26:39
memorization because if you have a list

00:26:36 --> 00:26:41
of things to memorize then fair enough

00:26:38 --> 00:26:43
you memorize a list of things but if you

00:26:40 --> 00:26:44
need to understand Concepts or if the

00:26:42 --> 00:26:46
kind of outline of what you need to know

00:26:43 --> 00:26:48
for your exam or for your studies isn't

00:26:45 --> 00:26:50
quite clear it's very important that we

00:26:47 --> 00:26:51
kind of create our own syllabus and in

00:26:49 --> 00:26:53
this video as said and I are discussing

00:26:50 --> 00:26:55
this concept a little bit further well I

00:26:52 --> 00:26:58
think in every case where you have such

00:26:54 --> 00:26:59
uncertainty I think you need to find a

00:26:57 --> 00:27:03
syllabus for yourself

00:26:58 --> 00:27:05
I think it's really counterproductive to

00:27:02 --> 00:27:08
not have a limit on what you're learning

00:27:04 --> 00:27:10
and there's always a tendency to try and

00:27:07 --> 00:27:12
expand the resources that you're that

00:27:09 --> 00:27:14
you're using because other people use

00:27:11 --> 00:27:16
different resources and they will

00:27:13 --> 00:27:17
yeah know things that you don't know the

00:27:15 --> 00:27:19
odds of handbook but there might there

00:27:16 --> 00:27:20
might be some Rogue fact and sort of the

00:27:18 --> 00:27:24
big pathology textbooks

00:27:19 --> 00:27:26
absolutely so I mean for me for instance

00:27:23 --> 00:27:28
I made the step one textbook my

00:27:25 --> 00:27:29
curriculum that I mean that definitely

00:27:27 --> 00:27:30
didn't have everything I had to

00:27:28 --> 00:27:33
supplement it with other things

00:27:29 --> 00:27:37
including including past medicine

00:27:32 --> 00:27:39
but that combination was incredibly

00:27:36 --> 00:27:41
comprehensive

00:27:38 --> 00:27:44
and yes there were things that I didn't

00:27:40 --> 00:27:47
know even like despite learning though

00:27:43 --> 00:27:50
learning sort of much of those

00:27:46 --> 00:27:52
but I think you have to accept the fact

00:27:49 --> 00:27:54
hat

00:27:51 --> 00:27:58
sort of you can't know everything and

00:27:53 --> 00:28:01
it's better to know a lot well

00:27:57 --> 00:28:03
then then sort of stress yourself into

00:28:00 --> 00:28:05
trying to achieve something which you

00:28:02 --> 00:28:06
can't really do yeah and I think that's

00:28:04 --> 00:28:09
a really important Insight that actually

00:28:05 --> 00:28:12
it's it's it's impossible to know

00:28:08 --> 00:28:13
everything and therefore what's the big

00:28:11 --> 00:28:15
chunk of information that you all know

00:28:12 --> 00:28:16
and what's the the sliver of information

00:28:14 --> 00:28:20
that you don't need to worry about yeah

00:28:15 --> 00:28:22
and in a way like simplifying like the

00:28:19 --> 00:28:24
more we simplify it the well in a way

00:28:21 --> 00:28:26
the easier it becomes I was I was having

00:28:23 --> 00:28:27
a chat with a student from UCL a couple

00:28:25 --> 00:28:28
of days ago

00:28:26 --> 00:28:30
um he was um in his fourth year and he

00:28:27 --> 00:28:31
was saying that yeah it just seems

00:28:29 --> 00:28:32
there's so much information I don't know

00:28:30 --> 00:28:33
hat to use I don't know if I should use

00:28:31 --> 00:28:35
Kumar and Clark Oxford handbook I don't

00:28:32 --> 00:28:37
know if I should use uh you know

00:28:34 --> 00:28:38
Alistair Scots UCL notes that have been

00:28:36 --> 00:28:42
propagated throughout the internet I

00:28:37 --> 00:28:43
don't know what to do and his it's it

00:28:41 --> 00:28:45
seemed like the advice that resonated

00:28:42 --> 00:28:47
with him when I gave it was that that he

00:28:45 --> 00:28:49
was thinking of clinical medicine as he

00:28:46 --> 00:28:52
was making it harder than it had to be

00:28:48 --> 00:28:53
like realistically all you basically

00:28:51 --> 00:28:55
have to know is what's in the Oxford

00:28:52 --> 00:28:57
handbook and then a few extra bits maybe

00:28:54 --> 00:28:59
to get a few years yeah and if you

00:28:56 --> 00:29:00
confine yourself to that you actually

00:28:58 --> 00:29:02
realize that oh well within Cardiology

00:28:59 --> 00:29:03
there's only 15 topics okay within

00:29:01 --> 00:29:05
respiratory there's only eight topics oh

00:29:03 --> 00:29:07
this is it becomes much more manageable

00:29:04 --> 00:29:09
yeah yeah I mean I will say the Oxford

handbook is really kind of sort of

poor in terms of explaining things yeah

00:29:11 --> 00:29:15
it tells you the stuff rather than helps

you understand the stuff and for that

00:29:14 --> 00:29:20
reason I almost I pretty much didn't use

00:29:16 --> 00:29:22
the Oxford handbook at all during my

clinical years

00:29:21 --> 00:29:27
um you the US Emily material was

00:29:23 --> 00:29:29
relatively a lot better explained okay I

00:29:26 --> 00:29:31
don't like the first aid step one for

00:29:28 --> 00:29:32
example yeah yeah

00:29:30 --> 00:29:36
um

00:29:31 --> 00:29:39
I think again the key is to take one or

00:29:35 --> 00:29:41
to take a few sets of a set of resources

00:29:38 --> 00:29:43
it might just be one thing or a couple

00:29:40 --> 00:29:45
of things and and make it your Canaan

00:29:42 --> 00:29:47
d really kind of

00:29:44 --> 00:29:49
master that

00:29:46 --> 00:29:51
and would you make notes on these things

to sort of compile into a master note

00:29:50 --> 00:29:55
yeah again it's pointless yeah because

00:29:52 --> 00:29:57
you've you've got the information there

00:29:54 --> 00:29:59
you need as in just as with the lecture

00:29:56 --> 00:30:01
notes in first and second yeah yeah why

00:29:58 --> 00:30:04
would you do this there was no point so

00:30:00 --> 00:30:05
yeah I'm just making redundant notes I I

00:30:03 --> 00:30:08
think once

00:30:04 --> 00:30:10
in third year

00:30:07 --> 00:30:12
um when I was doing essays in Theology

00:30:09 --> 00:30:15
and philosophy yeah

00:30:11 --> 00:30:19
each week whenever I was doing essays

00:30:14 --> 00:30:21
I'd have a big reading list and

00:30:18 --> 00:30:23
usually I mean these these topics again

00:30:20 --> 00:30:26
were highly abstract

00:30:22 --> 00:30:28
and what and it would usually take quite

00:30:25 --> 00:30:31
a while before I really understood what

00:30:27 --> 00:30:34
was going on and in each of those cases

00:30:30 --> 00:30:37
I found that if I could just take one

00:30:33 --> 00:30:39
kind of book what one of those books and

00:30:36 --> 00:30:41
make it kind of the key to my

00:30:38 --> 00:30:43
understanding really understand that

00:30:40 --> 00:30:46
book in detail I had to read it maybe

00:30:42 --> 00:30:47
three times in order to really

00:30:45 --> 00:30:50
understand what was going on

00:30:46 --> 00:30:52
when I got to that stage I could skim

00:30:49 --> 00:30:55
read the rest of the books and sort of

00:30:51 --> 00:30:57
basically top up the kind of

00:30:54 --> 00:30:59
oundational Concepts that I had and

00:30:56 --> 00:31:00
that would be really easy whereas I

00:30:58 --> 00:31:02
think if I'd read every book in turn I

00:30:59 --> 00:31:04
would have had a much more superficial

00:31:01 --> 00:31:06
understanding

00:31:03 --> 00:31:08
yeah so make one book your Bible and the

00:31:05 --> 00:31:11
rest are supplementary texts absolutely

00:31:07 --> 00:31:12
and and if you learn the Bible yeah the

00:31:10 --> 00:31:15
supplementary test you can get through

00:31:11 --> 00:31:16
sort of very quickly so that was a bit

00:31:14 --> 00:31:18
of discussion on how you can find a

00:31:15 --> 00:31:20
syllabus for yourself

00:31:17 --> 00:31:22
all right so welcome to the second

00:31:19 --> 00:31:25
segment of the course which is about

00:31:21 --> 00:31:27
remembering so segment number one or

00:31:24 --> 00:31:28
step one was understand step two is

00:31:26 --> 00:31:30
remember and as a reminder step three is

00:31:27 --> 00:31:32
focus so we'll talk a lot more about

00:31:29 --> 00:31:33
kind of distractions and motivations and

00:31:31 --> 00:31:35
procrastination all that kind of stuff

00:31:32 --> 00:31:37
in the focus segment but now we're going

00:31:34 --> 00:31:39
to be diving into all of the

00:31:36 --> 00:31:41
vidence-based tips for remembering

00:31:38 --> 00:31:43
slash memorizing things but again just

00:31:40 --> 00:31:45
o retreat understanding is the most

00:31:42 --> 00:31:46
important part and without understanding

00:31:44 --> 00:31:48
there's literally no point in memorizing

00:31:45 --> 00:31:49
or remembering stuff what's the problem

00:31:47 --> 00:31:51
here that we're trying to solve and as I

00:31:48 --> 00:31:52
mentioned in the previous section um

00:31:50 --> 00:31:54
what we're trying to do is we're trying

00:31:51 --> 00:31:56
to combat the forgetting curve so in the

00:31:53 --> 00:31:58
1800s there was a chap called ebbinghaus

00:31:55 --> 00:32:01
who did a study on himself where he made

00:31:57 --> 00:32:03
himself memorize all these random random

00:32:00 --> 00:32:06
words that he just kind of made up and

00:32:02 --> 00:32:08
he tested how quickly and how often he

00:32:05 --> 00:32:09
forgot what these words were and he

00:32:07 --> 00:32:12
realized that you know as soon as he

00:32:08 --> 00:32:14
learned a word his memory for that word

00:32:11 --> 00:32:17
ecayed exponentially over time

00:32:13 --> 00:32:18
but if he interrupted that forgetting by

00:32:16 --> 00:32:20
testing himself on the word again and

00:32:17 --> 00:32:22
then looking it up he interrupted that

00:32:19 --> 00:32:23
cover then he went back up to 100 but

00:32:21 --> 00:32:24
hen he found that crucially it took him

00:32:22 --> 00:32:26
longer to forget it the second time

00:32:23 --> 00:32:28
around but then when he forgot a little

00:32:25 --> 00:32:31
bit and he studied it again it took him

00:32:27 --> 00:32:34
even longer still so while the Decay is

00:32:30 --> 00:32:36
very precipitous to begin with the more

00:32:33 --> 00:32:37
times you repeat a topic the longer it

00:32:35 --> 00:32:40
hen takes for you to forget that

00:32:36 --> 00:32:42
information and this is the key Insight

00:32:39 --> 00:32:44
hat the whole of kind of the industry

00:32:41 --> 00:32:46
and the algorithms around space

00:32:43 --> 00:32:47
repetition are based around it's this

00:32:45 --> 00:32:49
idea that the more we repeat stuff the

00:32:46 --> 00:32:50
longer it takes to forget it but the

00:32:48 --> 00:32:53
longer it takes to forget it means that

00:32:49 --> 00:32:55
we should space our repetitions of that

00:32:52 --> 00:32:56
opic over time so let's say we're

00:32:54 --> 00:32:58
starting a topic on day one let's say

00:32:55 --> 00:33:00
we've studied I don't know thyroid

00:32:57 --> 00:33:01
function tests or the thyroid you know

00:32:59 --> 00:33:02
let's go for the thyroid let's keep it

00:33:00 --> 00:33:05
simple let's say we've studied the

00:33:01 --> 00:33:07
thyroid on like today by tomorrow we'll

00:33:04 --> 00:33:08
probably have forgotten about 50 of it

00:33:06 --> 00:33:10
and by three days we'd have forgotten

00:33:07 --> 00:33:12
like all of it because it decays

00:33:09 --> 00:33:13
exponentially in the early stages so

00:33:11 --> 00:33:16
let's say we revise it again tomorrow

00:33:12 --> 00:33:18
now our learning for it goes back to

00:33:15 --> 00:33:21
back up to 100 but now it takes let's

00:33:17 --> 00:33:23
ay a week to forget 50 of it so then in

00:33:20 --> 00:33:24
a week we come back to it and we study

00:33:22 --> 00:33:27
it and then let's say it takes a month

00:33:23 --> 00:33:29
to forget 50 of it so we've we've

00:33:26 --> 00:33:32
repeated it like a day a week and a

00:33:28 --> 00:33:33
month later but at spaced intervals and

00:33:31 --> 00:33:35
that's the idea behind space repetition

00:33:32 --> 00:33:37
so when I first discovered space

00:33:34 --> 00:33:38
repetition it was again in in the second

00:33:36 --> 00:33:40
year Medical School lecture on

00:33:37 --> 00:33:42
psychology which was all about the

00:33:39 --> 00:33:43
psychology of effective learning and

00:33:41 --> 00:33:45
this was one of the concepts that like

00:33:42 --> 00:33:46
when we first heard about it in that

00:33:44 --> 00:33:48
lecture everyone was like looking around

00:33:45 --> 00:33:50
in Wonder at one another because we were

00:33:47 --> 00:33:52
like oh my God this is absolutely game

00:33:49 --> 00:33:53
changing because in the past I'd always

00:33:51 --> 00:33:55
tried to make like these revision

00:33:52 --> 00:33:58
timetables but I just never really

00:33:54 --> 00:34:00
appreciated the fact that just by by

00:33:57 --> 00:34:01
virtue of the way memory Works my memory

00:33:59 --> 00:34:03
for everything was just going to Decay

00:34:00 --> 00:34:05
exponentially over time and so once I

00:34:03 --> 00:34:07
discovered the power space repetition I

00:34:04 --> 00:34:09
realized that I could scientifically

00:34:06 --> 00:34:10
choose when to revise certain things to

00:34:08 --> 00:34:12
maximize the chances that I'm going to

00:34:09 --> 00:34:13
retain them and what's also at play here

00:34:11 --> 00:34:15
is this idea that you know that theme

00:34:12 --> 00:34:16
that runs through this entire class

00:34:14 --> 00:34:18
which is that the more effort for

00:34:15 --> 00:34:20
learning feels the harder we have to

00:34:17 --> 00:34:21
work to learn something or to retrieve

00:34:19 --> 00:34:23
something the more likely that

00:34:20 --> 00:34:24
information is to stick so let's say I

00:34:22 --> 00:34:25
were to read about the thyroid today and

00:34:23 --> 00:34:26
then an hour later and then an hour

00:34:24 --> 00:34:29
later

00:34:25 --> 00:34:30
uh the the you know reading it like

00:34:28 --> 00:34:31
three times in one day would be a little

00:34:29 --> 00:34:33
bit pointless because I haven't

00:34:30 --> 00:34:34
forgotten any of it I haven't like

00:34:32 --> 00:34:36
allowed myself to forget any of it

00:34:33 --> 00:34:38
herefore my brain is not working to

00:34:35 --> 00:34:40
retain that information or to retrieve

00:34:37 --> 00:34:42
that information instead it's just a

00:34:39 --> 00:34:44
passive process and again like we've

00:34:41 --> 00:34:45
said many times before whenever learning

00:34:43 --> 00:34:47
anything or studying or revision becomes

00:34:44 --> 00:34:48
passive like you know we're playing the

00:34:46 --> 00:34:49
guitar and we're just kind of bashing

00:34:47 --> 00:34:52
out passively to songs we already know

00:34:48 --> 00:34:53
e are not learning anything at all it's

00:34:51 --> 00:34:55
when learning is hard like when we're

00:34:52 --> 00:34:57
playing those new chords of that new

00:34:54 --> 00:34:59
fingering or whatever that sounds weird

00:34:56 --> 00:35:01
um in the guitar on the piano it's when

00:34:58 --> 00:35:03
it's difficult that we actually learn

00:35:00 --> 00:35:04
and we can consolidate that information

00:35:02 --> 00:35:06
it's the same with learning anything for

00:35:03 --> 00:35:07
exams or whatever you're learning the

00:35:05 --> 00:35:09
more difficult it is the more strongly

00:35:06 --> 00:35:10
that information is going to stick and I

00:35:08 --> 00:35:12
will keep on repeating that every single

00:35:09 --> 00:35:13
video pretty much but now let's move on

00:35:11 --> 00:35:15
to talk about some of the ways in which

00:35:12 --> 00:35:17
we can practically apply the concept of

00:35:14 --> 00:35:19
space repetition to our own studying to

00:35:16 --> 00:35:21
supercharge our productivity and to make

00:35:18 --> 00:35:23
sure we remember the stuff that we need

00:35:20 --> 00:35:25
to remember

00:35:22 --> 00:35:26
Okay so we've talked about why space

00:35:24 --> 00:35:28
repetition is an absolutely amazing

00:35:25 --> 00:35:30
thing and let's now talk about some of

00:35:27 --> 00:35:32
the methods for applying space

00:35:29 --> 00:35:33
repetition to our studying and my

00:35:31 --> 00:35:36
personal favorite way of doing this is

00:35:33 --> 00:35:38
by using the retrospective revision

00:35:35 --> 00:35:41
timetable now this is a term that I've

00:35:37 --> 00:35:42
coined it's basically it's basically a

00:35:40 --> 00:35:44
revision timetable but instead of

00:35:41 --> 00:35:46
deciding in advance what topics we're

00:35:43 --> 00:35:48
going to do in any given day we decide

00:35:45 --> 00:35:51
on the day based on what topics we've

00:35:47 --> 00:35:52
done in the past and how effective our

00:35:50 --> 00:35:54
ecall of that topic was I will just

00:35:51 --> 00:35:56
show you how I actually use that in

00:35:53 --> 00:35:58
otion these days so here we go this is

00:35:55 --> 00:35:59
hom hummus stands for homeostasis which

00:35:57 --> 00:36:02
is human physiology in the Cambridge

00:35:58 --> 00:36:03
course and this is what I've done

00:36:01 --> 00:36:05
retrospective revision timetable wise so

00:36:03 --> 00:36:07
you can see we've got the topic of

00:36:04 --> 00:36:08
nerves and then on the first of January

00:36:06 --> 00:36:10
I did that for the first time and then

00:36:07 --> 00:36:12
on the second and then on the 7th of

00:36:09 --> 00:36:14
January and then on the 20th of Feb and

00:36:11 --> 00:36:15
there's different ways that we can rate

00:36:13 --> 00:36:17
our understanding of it so when I was

00:36:14 --> 00:36:18
using Google Sheets I would just color

00:36:16 --> 00:36:20
in the cell using I don't know the

00:36:17 --> 00:36:22
coloring feature unfortunately on notion

00:36:19 --> 00:36:24
you can't color in the cells of a table

00:36:21 --> 00:36:26
so instead usually

00:36:23 --> 00:36:27
um if I'm not feeling fancy I'll just

00:36:25 --> 00:36:29
rate it out of five so this one was a

00:36:26 --> 00:36:31
three out of five four out of five and

00:36:28 --> 00:36:32
five out of five but sometimes if I'm

00:36:30 --> 00:36:34
feeling fancy I'll traffic light it with

00:36:31 --> 00:36:36
Emojis so you can use red yellow green

00:36:33 --> 00:36:38
whatever or you can use stars like two

00:36:35 --> 00:36:40
star four star five star but essentially

00:36:37 --> 00:36:42
it's exactly the same thing it just lets

00:36:39 --> 00:36:44
me see on a given day what topic I want

00:36:41 --> 00:36:45
o study so for example today is the

00:36:43 --> 00:36:47
28th of February I think 27th of

00:36:45 --> 00:36:50
February and so I'd have done renaline

00:36:46 --> 00:36:50
respiratory recently

00:36:49 --> 00:36:53
um

00:36:49 --> 00:36:55
I did nerves last on the 20th of Feb and

00:36:52 --> 00:36:56
so I'll probably do that one again

00:36:54 --> 00:36:57
actually

00:36:55 --> 00:36:59
um

00:36:56 --> 00:37:00
no I'll probably do renal again so then

00:36:58 --> 00:37:02
that takes it up to three repetitions of

00:36:59 --> 00:37:04
renal so we'll do 27 Feb there and

00:37:01 --> 00:37:06
hopefully then that might be like a

00:37:03 --> 00:37:06
three out of five or I think you know

00:37:05 --> 00:37:08
I've done it three times recently

00:37:05 --> 00:37:09
probably four out of five and then next

00:37:07 --> 00:37:11
ime I come around to studying

00:37:08 --> 00:37:13
physiology I can decide based on the

00:37:10 --> 00:37:15
retrospective revision timetable exactly

00:37:12 --> 00:37:17
what I want to be doing so that's one

00:37:14 --> 00:37:18
way of going about how to incorporate

00:37:16 --> 00:37:20
space repetition

00:37:17 --> 00:37:21
all right so in the last video we talked

00:37:19 --> 00:37:23
about my retrospective revision

00:37:21 --> 00:37:26
timetable method for incorporating

00:37:22 --> 00:37:27
spaced repetition into my life but a few

00:37:25 --> 00:37:28
eeks ago I was chatting to my friend

00:37:26 --> 00:37:31
Simon clock we were doing a collab video

00:37:27 --> 00:37:32
n YouTube and he mentioned his his

00:37:30 --> 00:37:34
method of doing this which is called the

00:37:31 --> 00:37:36
spaced repetition journal or space

00:37:33 --> 00:37:38
repetition diary and we included this in

00:37:35 --> 00:37:40
a YouTube video but I will include the

00:37:37 --> 00:37:42
uncut conversation between me and Simon

00:37:39 --> 00:37:44
in this in this course now uh so you can

00:37:41 --> 00:37:46
see us discussing this concept of the

00:37:43 --> 00:37:47
space repetition diary and you'll hear

00:37:45 --> 00:37:50
Simon explaining how this method Works

00:37:46 --> 00:37:52
uh I get that some people like the idea

00:37:49 --> 00:37:54
of the kind of pre-deciding what they're

00:37:51 --> 00:37:55
going to study because I mean it kind of

00:37:53 --> 00:37:56
makes sense then on a given day you

00:37:54 --> 00:37:58
don't really have to think about it you

00:37:55 --> 00:38:00
can just execute on the plan that you've

00:37:57 --> 00:38:01
already made and I think Simon's method

00:37:59 --> 00:38:04
is a good way of building a revision

00:38:00 --> 00:38:05
timetable but based on kind of the

00:38:03 --> 00:38:06
principle of space repetition rather

00:38:04 --> 00:38:08
than kind of doing it randomly like I

00:38:05 --> 00:38:10
used to do when I was in school so here

00:38:07 --> 00:38:12
is now me and Simon talking about his

00:38:09 --> 00:38:14
paced repetition diary if I could

00:38:11 --> 00:38:16
travel back in time and slap two words

00:38:13 --> 00:38:19
into 10 year old Simon's head it they

00:38:15 --> 00:38:21
would be spaced repetition like cannot

00:38:18 --> 00:38:22
stress enough how important this

00:38:20 --> 00:38:23
technique is to give you an example when

00:38:21 --> 00:38:25
I was doing my third year exams at

00:38:22 --> 00:38:27
Oxford and it was the best like academic

00:38:24 --> 00:38:29
performance of my academic career did

00:38:26 --> 00:38:31
really well I think the reason for it

00:38:28 --> 00:38:33
was I used this technique where I would

00:38:30 --> 00:38:36
on a given day if I studied a particular

00:38:32 --> 00:38:39
subject write in a paper diary I did on

00:38:35 --> 00:38:41
say the first of May uh Atomic physics

00:38:38 --> 00:38:43
looking at a particular subtopic I then

00:38:40 --> 00:38:45
go forward a day and write down that

00:38:42 --> 00:38:46
opic again go forward a week write that

00:38:44 --> 00:38:49
opic go forward a month and write that

00:38:45 --> 00:38:51
opic and then as you went through your

00:38:48 --> 00:38:54
evision you gradually populated this

00:38:50 --> 00:38:56
diary with the revision that you needed

00:38:53 --> 00:38:59
to go back and look at on a given day so

00:38:55 --> 00:39:01
you get to say I don't know May 14th and

00:38:58 --> 00:39:03
you would say right well I now need to

00:39:00 --> 00:39:05
revise this part of atomic physics this

00:39:02 --> 00:39:07
part of general relativity this part of

00:39:04 --> 00:39:09
subatomic physics and you would have a

00:39:06 --> 00:39:11
ready-made checklist of stuff to go over

00:39:08 --> 00:39:13
again as well as the stuff that you had

00:39:10 --> 00:39:15
to look forward to in your revision plan

00:39:12 --> 00:39:17
d it meant that I covered everything

00:39:14 --> 00:39:19
several times at these increasing

00:39:16 --> 00:39:22
intervals and it just stayed in my head

00:39:18 --> 00:39:24
so much better but it was the ease at

00:39:21 --> 00:39:25
which I could Rock up to the library

00:39:23 --> 00:39:28
open up my diary and say right what's

00:39:24 --> 00:39:30
today it's the 14th of May okay I've got

00:39:27 --> 00:39:34
hese five topics to do first let's go

00:39:29 --> 00:39:35
and it just made life so much easier and

00:39:33 --> 00:39:37
I did so much better because of it

00:39:34 --> 00:39:39
alright so that was Simon Clark's uh

prospective uh spaced repetition diary

size Journal thing I think it's pretty

ay on the 25th of February I read up

00:40:58 --> 00:41:02
but anyway that was Simon Clark's space

00:40:59 --> 00:41:03
repetition Journal slash diary I hope

00:41:01 --> 00:41:05
you found that useful let's now move on

00:41:02 --> 00:41:07
to the next video

00:41:04 --> 00:41:09
all right so we've spent a bit of time

00:41:06 --> 00:41:11
talking about space repetition which is

00:41:08 --> 00:41:13
the idea that you leave time in between

00:41:10 --> 00:41:15
study sessions let's now talk about

00:41:12 --> 00:41:17
interleaving which is which involves how

00:41:14 --> 00:41:19
you split up your time within one study

00:41:16 --> 00:41:21
session itself and interleaving is the

00:41:18 --> 00:41:23
idea that we should mix up our practice

00:41:20 --> 00:41:25
as we go along within the same study

00:41:22 --> 00:41:26
session because the idea is that again

00:41:24 --> 00:41:28
returning to the thread that's running

00:41:25 --> 00:41:30
through this whole course that whenever

00:41:27 --> 00:41:32
stuff becomes too easy whenever we're

00:41:29 --> 00:41:34
not working very hard whenever our brain

00:41:31 --> 00:41:35
is lifting very lightweights then at

00:41:33 --> 00:41:37
hat point we stop learning as

00:41:34 --> 00:41:38
effectively and so the idea behind

00:41:36 --> 00:41:39
interleaving is that we do a little bit

00:41:37 --> 00:41:41
of one topic and a little bit of another

00:41:39 --> 00:41:43
topic and then a little bit of a third

00:41:40 --> 00:41:45
topic and then back to the first rather

00:41:42 --> 00:41:46
than do three hours on one topic three

00:41:44 --> 00:41:48
hours on topic two and three hours on

00:41:45 --> 00:41:50
topic three or whatever your timing ends

00:41:47 --> 00:41:51
up being and there's a fair bit of

00:41:49 --> 00:41:54
evidence that interleaving improves

00:41:51 --> 00:41:55
retention when it comes to studying for

00:41:53 --> 00:41:57
exams but also when it comes to learning

00:41:54 --> 00:41:59
anything so again in the book make it

00:41:56 --> 00:42:00
stick which I don't have a physical copy

00:41:58 --> 00:42:01
of because I read it on Kindle but you

00:41:59 --> 00:42:03
should definitely read that they talk

00:42:00 --> 00:42:05
about this famous hockey coach who like

00:42:02 --> 00:42:07
he gets his players to do certain

00:42:04 --> 00:42:08
exercises and then just as they're

00:42:06 --> 00:42:10
getting the hang of those exercises he

00:42:07 --> 00:42:11
switches them onto something else and

00:42:09 --> 00:42:12
just as they get the hang of that he

00:42:10 --> 00:42:14
switches them onto something else and

00:42:12 --> 00:42:15
the players don't really like this

00:42:13 --> 00:42:16
because they have to work a bit harder

00:42:14 --> 00:42:18
and you know just as they're approaching

00:42:15 --> 00:42:20
the Mastery and they're like oh yeah I

00:42:17 --> 00:42:22
can do this this kind of makes sense he

00:42:19 --> 00:42:23
moves them onto something else but he

00:42:21 --> 00:42:24
gets results and the teams end up

00:42:22 --> 00:42:27
winning like loads of stuff and the

00:42:23 --> 00:42:28
players are happy for it in hindsight

00:42:26 --> 00:42:31
because they recognize that actually

00:42:27 --> 00:42:32
it's only when stuff is difficult that's

00:42:30 --> 00:42:34
when we're actually learning it and the

00:42:31 --> 00:42:37
ory behind this as they say in the

00:42:33 --> 00:42:39
study is that when we are blocking stuff

00:42:36 --> 00:42:41
like for example if we're doing math

00:42:38 --> 00:42:42
problems and we're doing lots of the

00:42:40 --> 00:42:44
same problem at once just changing the

00:42:41 --> 00:42:45
numbers our brain starts to take

00:42:43 --> 00:42:47
shortcuts it starts to realize okay

00:42:44 --> 00:42:48
that's the method and then it just

00:42:46 --> 00:42:50
arts applying that method but if we're

00:42:48 --> 00:42:52
doing different sorts of problems that

00:42:49 --> 00:42:54
are all mixed up our brain has to take

00:42:51 --> 00:42:55
that initial step of thinking okay what

00:42:53 --> 00:42:57
method do I have to use to solve this

00:42:54 --> 00:42:59
problem and the Very fact that our brain

00:42:56 --> 00:43:00
requires that extra step means that

00:42:58 --> 00:43:02
we're more likely to perform better on

00:42:59 --> 00:43:03
the exam when the exam is given later on

00:43:01 --> 00:43:05
I think it's just something worth

00:43:02 --> 00:43:07
keeping the back of our minds that we

00:43:04 --> 00:43:09
want studying to be hard and you know

00:43:06 --> 00:43:10
like the analogy of going to the gym you

00:43:08 --> 00:43:12
don't want it to be easy if it's easy

00:43:09 --> 00:43:13
then yeah sure you might be having fun

00:43:11 --> 00:43:15
but you're not actually boosting your

00:43:12 --> 00:43:17
muscles just like if studying is easy it

00:43:15 --> 00:43:19
might be a bit more fun but we're not

00:43:16 --> 00:43:20
actually improving our brain performance

00:43:18 --> 00:43:22
so yeah that's interleaving and let's

00:43:19 --> 00:43:24
move on to the next video

00:43:21 --> 00:43:26
all right so welcome back again this is

00:43:23 --> 00:43:28
ection two of this class we'll be

00:43:25 --> 00:43:29
talking about how to remember stuff and

00:43:27 --> 00:43:31
in this video I want to talk about the

00:43:28 --> 00:43:32
idea of re-reading our notes now there

00:43:30 --> 00:43:34
have been loads of studies that

00:43:31 --> 00:43:36
basically show you know spoiler alert

00:43:33 --> 00:43:38
hat re-reading is probably a waste of

00:43:35 --> 00:43:40
time so for example that 2013 paper from

00:43:37 --> 00:43:42
Professor donlowski and his colleagues

00:43:39 --> 00:43:44
concluded that reading is low utility

00:43:41 --> 00:43:46
and he says and I quote when compared

00:43:43 --> 00:43:48
with some other learning techniques

00:43:45 --> 00:43:50
rereading is typically much less

00:43:47 --> 00:43:52
effective the relative disadvantage of

00:43:49 --> 00:43:53
rereading to other techniques is the

00:43:51 --> 00:43:55
largest strike against rereading and is

00:43:52 --> 00:43:56
the factor that weighed most heavily in

00:43:54 --> 00:43:58
our decision to assign it a rating of

00:43:55 --> 00:43:59
low utility

00:43:57 --> 00:44:02
so that's a pretentious fancy way of

00:43:58 --> 00:44:03
saying that yeah rereading does work but

00:44:01 --> 00:44:05
here are so many better things we could

00:44:02 --> 00:44:07
be doing instead like active recall and

00:44:04 --> 00:44:08
space repetition and stuff and

00:44:06 --> 00:44:11
interleaving and categorizing and things

00:44:07 --> 00:44:12
that we'll talk about more later so how

00:44:10 --> 00:44:15
can re-reading actually be effective

00:44:11 --> 00:44:16
well I don't really think it can I mean

00:44:14 --> 00:44:19
I still reread my notes to this day some

00:44:15 --> 00:44:20
of the time but whenever I do it's

00:44:18 --> 00:44:22
because I have a very small amount of

00:44:19 --> 00:44:23
energy and because I can't be bothered

00:44:21 --> 00:44:25
to do something that's more efficient

00:44:22 --> 00:44:27
and let's say it's 11 pm and we haven't

00:44:24 --> 00:44:28
had a chance to do any studying the

00:44:26 --> 00:44:29
whole day and we're really really tired

00:44:27 --> 00:44:31
but we want to get that rep in we want

00:44:28 --> 00:44:34
o make sure we've done some studying

00:44:30 --> 00:44:35
today then yeah you know I as much as

00:44:33 --> 00:44:36
anyone else fall into that trap of

00:44:34 --> 00:44:38
thinking you know what I'm just going to

00:44:35 --> 00:44:39
read my notes but I know it doesn't

00:44:37 --> 00:44:41
actually do anything it doesn't actually

00:44:38 --> 00:44:43
help me but at that at that point I

00:44:40 --> 00:44:44
can't bring myself to muster of the

00:44:42 --> 00:44:46
nergy to do something more effective

00:44:43 --> 00:44:48
that requires more hard work so in that

00:44:45 --> 00:44:50
sense I think rereading is sort of

00:44:47 --> 00:44:52
useful it's useful to have as as the

00:44:49 --> 00:44:53
quivalent of going to the gym doing a

00:44:51 --> 00:44:55
single pull-up and then just going on

00:44:52 --> 00:44:57
your phone for the rest of the time it's

00:44:54 --> 00:44:58
that useful in my opinion having tried

00:44:56 --> 00:44:59
it a lot of a lot of the time I've been

00:44:57 --> 00:45:01
realizing that actually active recall is

00:44:58 --> 00:45:02
o much better but it's nice it's nice

00:45:00 --> 00:45:04
to have that option when we have low

00:45:01 --> 00:45:06
energy levels so moral of the story we

00:45:03 --> 00:45:07
probably shouldn't be rereading but hey

00:45:05 --> 00:45:09
if rereading works for you and you're

00:45:06 --> 00:45:10
very happy with your results then I'm

00:45:08 --> 00:45:11
not sure why you're watching this course

00:45:09 --> 00:45:12
because you probably won't learn

00:45:10 --> 00:45:14
anything but if you're doing lots of

00:45:11 --> 00:45:15
rereading and you're unhappy with your

00:45:13 --> 00:45:17
esults like you would rather get better

00:45:14 --> 00:45:18
esults in less time then at that point

00:45:16 --> 00:45:20
you probably want to start using

00:45:17 --> 00:45:22
something like active recall and space

00:45:19 --> 00:45:25
repetition rather than rereading

00:45:21 --> 00:45:26
all right so in this remembering section

00:45:24 --> 00:45:27
we've talked about active recall like

00:45:25 --> 00:45:28
testing ourselves as the single best

00:45:26 --> 00:45:30
hing ever we've talked about space

00:45:27 --> 00:45:32
repetition and we've talked about my

00:45:29 --> 00:45:34
retrospective revision timetable and my

00:45:31 --> 00:45:36
friend Simon's prospective spaced

00:45:33 --> 00:45:37
repetition diary slash Journal we've

00:45:35 --> 00:45:39
talked about interleaving and wides good

00:45:36 --> 00:45:41
and we've talked about re-reading and

why it's bad let's now talk about

00:45:40 --> 00:45:45
highlighting now highlighting is one of

00:45:42 --> 00:45:46
those things that we all love to do I

00:45:44 --> 00:45:48
love carrying different colored

00:45:45 --> 00:45:51
highlighters with me because it makes me

00:45:47 --> 00:45:52
feel Pro you know like I remember when

my mum Was preparing for her medical

00:45:51 --> 00:45:55
exams and I would watch her studying

00:45:52 --> 00:45:56
she'd be like highlighting with this one

00:45:54 --> 00:45:58
of these like Stabler highlighter

00:45:55 --> 00:45:59
s and I'd see her book with

00:45:58 --> 00:46:02

00:46:00 --> 00:46:05
this is what studying is supposed to

00:46:01 --> 00:46:06
look like but unfortunately if we look

00:46:04 --> 00:46:08
at the evidence highlighting is not very

00:46:05 --> 00:46:09
effective so and there's that study from

00:46:07 --> 00:46:11
2013 that I've been referencing a lot

00:46:08 --> 00:46:12
from Professor donloski and his

00:46:10 --> 00:46:13
colleagues and they looked at

00:46:11 --> 00:46:15
highlighting and all the evidence around

00:46:12 --> 00:46:17
highlighting and they concluded that

00:46:14 --> 00:46:18
highlighting is low utility I.E it's not

00:46:16 --> 00:46:21
very good

00:46:17 --> 00:46:23
um there is one study from 1974 which

00:46:20 --> 00:46:26
says that active High lighting is

00:46:22 --> 00:46:27
uperior to passive re-reading of

00:46:25 --> 00:46:28
highlighted material like you know if

00:46:26 --> 00:46:30
you're reading stuff and you're

00:46:27 --> 00:46:32
highlighting it as you go along that's

00:46:29 --> 00:46:34
better than just reading stuff that's

00:46:31 --> 00:46:35
already been highlighted so for me

00:46:33 --> 00:46:37
personally highlighting doesn't detract

00:46:34 --> 00:46:39
from the Feynman technique and from the

00:46:36 --> 00:46:40
active recall that I'm doing it sort of

00:46:38 --> 00:46:42
helps create active recall questions

00:46:39 --> 00:46:43
further down the line but I think for me

00:46:41 --> 00:46:45
the most important fact is that

00:46:42 --> 00:46:46
highlighting just helps me concentrate a

00:46:44 --> 00:46:48
little bit better and makes my notes

00:46:45 --> 00:46:50
look pretty which means when I do a

top-down Instagram shot of them it looks

00:46:49 --> 00:46:53
like I've done a little bit of work

00:46:50 --> 00:46:54
rather than just have a blank slate so

00:46:52 --> 00:46:56
yeah those are my thoughts on

00:46:53 --> 00:46:58
highlighting sorry Professor dunloski I

00:46:55 --> 00:47:00
know you say it's not very good but I

enjoy it it's fun makes me feel happy so

00:46:59 --> 00:47:04
that's highlighting

00:47:01 --> 00:47:06
all right so whenever anyone first finds

00:47:03 --> 00:47:07
out about active recall and space

00:47:05 --> 00:47:09
repetition

00:47:06 --> 00:47:11
um there's one concept that seems to

00:47:08 --> 00:47:14
always crop up and that is flashcards

00:47:10 --> 00:47:15
because flashcards are a marriage a

00:47:13 --> 00:47:17
marriage of active recall and space

00:47:15 --> 00:47:19
repetition

00:47:16 --> 00:47:20
so what is a flash card flashcard is

00:47:18 --> 00:47:22
basically let's say you have a piece of

00:47:19 --> 00:47:23
paper I'm going to use this trackpad as

00:47:21 --> 00:47:25
example and at the front of the

00:47:22 --> 00:47:28
flashcard you would have something like

00:47:24 --> 00:47:29
capital of Peru and on the back of the

00:47:27 --> 00:47:31
flashcard you have the answer so you

00:47:28 --> 00:47:32
might write lever for example so the

00:47:30 --> 00:47:33
idea is that you have a question at the

00:47:31 --> 00:47:36
front and then at the back you would put

00:47:33 --> 00:47:38
your notes now lots of people in the

00:47:35 --> 00:47:39
past used to use physical flashcards and

00:47:37 --> 00:47:41
there was something called the Lightner

00:47:38 --> 00:47:43
system to help you kind of induce space

00:47:40 --> 00:47:45
repetition with that I think physical

00:47:42 --> 00:47:47
flashcards are a total waste of time now

00:47:44 --> 00:47:49
because we've got apps that are really

00:47:46 --> 00:47:51
good for effectively doing

00:47:48 --> 00:47:52
flashcards so I'd recommend you download

00:47:50 --> 00:47:54
the app Anki and you can use that

00:47:51 --> 00:47:56
alternatively there is Quizlet and if

00:47:53 --> 00:47:58
you pay for Quizlet premium which costs

00:47:55 --> 00:48:01
like 20 quid a year I think then you get

00:47:57 --> 00:48:03
he space repetition built into that but

00:48:00 --> 00:48:05
why is Anki so good and I'm going to

00:48:02 --> 00:48:08
show you why anki's so Good by showing

00:48:04 --> 00:48:10
you on here how Anki works so here's

00:48:07 --> 00:48:13
what Anki looks like basically you have

00:48:09 --> 00:48:16
decks of cards and then

00:48:12 --> 00:48:17
you can do the flashcards and what you

can do is you can either make your own

cards or you can download a deck off the

internet most people recommend making

your own cards unless you're studying I

00:48:23 --> 00:48:28
think I think with medicine specifically

depending on what exam you're doing for

00:48:27 --> 00:48:32
example if you're taking the USMLE and

you're following a resource like pathomo

which is like a really common resource

that everyone uses to Prep Pro to

00:48:34 --> 00:48:40
prepare for it then other people have

00:48:36 --> 00:48:42
already made Anki decks based off of

pathoma so there's not much point in you

in us like creating our own flashcards

00:48:43 --> 00:48:47
for that because the work's already been

done but I think in most other subjects

it does it does make a lot of sense to

obviously we're engaging with the

information better it's a bit harder and

therefore our brain's more likely to

00:48:53 --> 00:48:57
remember it downloading a deck of the

00:48:55 --> 00:49:00
internet is easy it's sort of the lazy

00:48:56 --> 00:49:01
option but it is it is doable so here is

00:48:59 --> 00:49:04
um some Decks that I've I've been making

as I've been going along studying for

one of my exams and I am not necessarily

using flashcards the right way in fact

let's do this one um so the MRCP is the

ncoded for by genes on chromosome six

yes I got it right I can't believe that

was straight out of my head easy

HLA something are class 1 antigens well

actually something across two antigens

00:50:09 --> 00:50:14
I don't know I don't know what this is

are class 1 antigens while DP DQ and Dr

but I didn't know the answer so I'm

again in three minutes time so you can

see this is forcing me to do active

recall by testing me on on like the

knowledge of this stuff but it's also

because if I got something wrong it

00:50:33 --> 00:50:37
again in three minutes or in 15 minutes

or or whatever and if I got something

right it can tell it to show me it

00:50:38 --> 00:50:42
showed to show it to me again in four
