days and if I got that question about

00:50:41 --> 00:50:46
Von hippo lindau Syndrome again in four

00:50:43 --> 00:50:48
days time and I got it right again then

00:50:45 --> 00:50:51
the easy interval would change to like a

00:50:47 --> 00:50:53
month and so Anki has an algorithm for

00:50:50 --> 00:50:56
space repetition built in so that the

00:50:52 --> 00:50:59
harder cards come more often and the

00:50:55 --> 00:51:01
asier cards come less often like you

00:50:58 --> 00:51:02
know you would actually space the

00:51:00 --> 00:51:03
intervals out

um and there's been loads of people on

00:51:02 --> 00:51:07
the internet who have

00:51:03 --> 00:51:08
dug down into the maths and the science

00:51:06 --> 00:51:10
behind the Anki space repetition

00:51:07 --> 00:51:11
algorithm so I'll put links to some of

00:51:09 --> 00:51:13
those things in the in the video

00:51:10 --> 00:51:15
description Anki is really really

00:51:12 --> 00:51:17
popular in medical school because you

00:51:14 --> 00:51:19
just have a huge turn of information to

00:51:16 --> 00:51:21
memorize and so for the people that use

00:51:18 --> 00:51:23
Anki they swear by it but one of the

00:51:20 --> 00:51:26
problems with Anki is that to use it

00:51:22 --> 00:51:28
well and to use it properly ideally you

00:51:25 --> 00:51:31
want to keep it to one fact per card

00:51:27 --> 00:51:33
rather than just copying or pasting all

00:51:30 --> 00:51:34
your notes on a single card and ideally

00:51:32 --> 00:51:36
ou want to be doing it consistently

00:51:33 --> 00:51:38
over a long period of time so I love

00:51:35 --> 00:51:40
using flashcards if I start early on and

00:51:37 --> 00:51:42
I can do it for isolated facts so yeah

00:51:39 --> 00:51:44
in an Ideal World we would have one

00:51:41 --> 00:51:45
xaminable fact per flash card and we'd

00:51:43 --> 00:51:47
be doing it very very consistently but

00:51:44 --> 00:51:48
here are other ways of using flashcards

00:51:46 --> 00:51:50
o for example in my third year the Year

00:51:47 --> 00:51:51
in which I ranked first in the year or

00:51:49 --> 00:51:53
ather second in the year but won the

00:51:50 --> 00:51:54
prize for Best exam performance jointly

00:51:52 --> 00:51:56
with this other girl who came first

00:51:53 --> 00:51:58
anyway uh ranked really highly there

00:51:55 --> 00:51:59
um and well I was using flash cards

00:51:57 --> 00:52:01
extensively for that but in my third

00:51:58 --> 00:52:03
year I wasn't using flashcards for

00:52:00 --> 00:52:04
single examinable facts because it was

00:52:02 --> 00:52:07
entirely essay based I was studying

00:52:03 --> 00:52:09
ecology and our exam was 100 essays so

00:52:06 --> 00:52:10
we had to write essays about psychology

00:52:08 --> 00:52:13
and so what I was using Anki for is that

00:52:09 --> 00:52:15
I was using it to memorize different

00:52:12 --> 00:52:17
chunks of content so that I could

00:52:14 --> 00:52:20
basically drag and drop them from my

00:52:16 --> 00:52:22
mind into the essay in the exam so for

00:52:19 --> 00:52:24
example on the front of the flashcard I

00:52:21 --> 00:52:26
would write I don't know karpuki and

00:52:23 --> 00:52:28
blunt 1972 and that would be a reference

00:52:25 --> 00:52:30
to a paper and on the back of the flash

00:52:27 --> 00:52:32
card it would be an explanation of what

00:52:29 --> 00:52:33
hey found and what the papers showed

00:52:31 --> 00:52:35
and what the results were and why it's

00:52:32 --> 00:52:37
relevant to me and so I would I would

00:52:34 --> 00:52:39
have lots and lots of facts on a single

00:52:36 --> 00:52:41
flash card but I would if you then told

00:52:38 --> 00:52:44
me you know at the time copycan blood in

00:52:40 --> 00:52:45
1972 I would instantly know what that

00:52:43 --> 00:52:47
study was what they found and what essay

00:52:45 --> 00:52:49
I could I could put it in and this is

00:52:46 --> 00:52:50
technically not what you're supposed to

00:52:48 --> 00:52:53
do when you're doing flashcards because

00:52:49 --> 00:52:54
ideally you want to have one mem one

00:52:52 --> 00:52:56
xaminable fact per flashcard but I

00:52:54 --> 00:52:58
think if you have got an essay exam then

00:52:55 --> 00:53:01
memorizing whole chunks of content like

00:52:57 --> 00:53:02
ven for some of my essays I'd prepared

00:53:00 --> 00:53:04
really fancy nicely written

00:53:01 --> 00:53:05
introductions and conclusions and I put

00:53:03 --> 00:53:07
all of those into Anki so I would

00:53:04 --> 00:53:08
memorize introductions for my essays and

00:53:06 --> 00:53:10
I'll talk a lot more about this in the

00:53:07 --> 00:53:11
video called the essay memorization

00:53:09 --> 00:53:13
framework which again I've put on

00:53:10 --> 00:53:14
YouTube which is actually one of my most

00:53:12 --> 00:53:16
popular videos so that talks in depth

00:53:13 --> 00:53:18
about how I used Anki to memorize chunks

00:53:15 --> 00:53:21
for essays I just wanted to flag up that

00:53:17 --> 00:53:22
is a potential use of Anki oh

00:53:20 --> 00:53:23
flashcards in general if you want to go

00:53:21 --> 00:53:24
the handwritten route but I don't know

00:53:22 --> 00:53:26
hy you'd want to do that but again on

00:53:23 --> 00:53:28
the topic of flashcards let's talk about

00:53:25 --> 00:53:30
a few cautionary tales so what you don't

00:53:27 --> 00:53:31
want to do is you do not want to make a

00:53:29 --> 00:53:33
flash card for absolutely everything

00:53:30 --> 00:53:34
because then we get into flashcard

00:53:32 --> 00:53:36
overload and there are so many people

00:53:33 --> 00:53:38
like I I had this problem in my first

00:53:35 --> 00:53:41
year like I discovered Anki towards the

00:53:37 --> 00:53:42
nd of my first year and I sort of like

00:53:40 --> 00:53:44
for for about two weeks I was making

00:53:41 --> 00:53:45
flashcards on absolutely everything

00:53:43 --> 00:53:47
thinking oh this is efficient why don't

00:53:44 --> 00:53:49
I make flash cards but then I realized

00:53:46 --> 00:53:50
very quickly that actually this is too

00:53:48 --> 00:53:51
many flashcards and I can't go through

00:53:49 --> 00:53:53
them so I ended up going back to my

00:53:50 --> 00:53:54
other techniques but a friend of mine

00:53:52 --> 00:53:55
called Catherine I ended up making

00:53:53 --> 00:53:58
flashcards for absolutely everything

00:53:54 --> 00:54:00
like for the whole year and she had like

00:53:57 --> 00:54:01
5 000 flash cards by the end of the year

00:53:59 --> 00:54:03
and she realized that there was no way

00:54:00 --> 00:54:05
she could feasibly go through all 5000

00:54:02 --> 00:54:06
flashcards and she felt that all that

00:54:04 --> 00:54:09
effort was was a bit of a waste of time

00:54:05 --> 00:54:11
so what I do now when making flashcards

00:54:08 --> 00:54:13
is that I only make flash cards for

00:54:10 --> 00:54:16
something if I absolutely have to

00:54:12 --> 00:54:17
um so if I'm making my own flashcard I

00:54:15 --> 00:54:19
don't usually make them in my first pass

00:54:16 --> 00:54:21
through the content usually I use the

00:54:18 --> 00:54:23
Cornell note-taking system for that but

00:54:20 --> 00:54:26
if there's a factor or a concept that's

00:54:22 --> 00:54:27
proving resistant to memorizing or

00:54:25 --> 00:54:29
emembering or whatever or that I find

00:54:26 --> 00:54:31
particularly tricky then I will make an

00:54:28 --> 00:54:33
Anki flashcard out of it or for example

00:54:30 --> 00:54:35
if I'm doing past papers on you know a

00:54:32 --> 00:54:36
website like past medicine or past test

00:54:34 --> 00:54:39
which is an online question bank for

00:54:35 --> 00:54:41
medical school for medical students if I

00:54:38 --> 00:54:43
get something wrong then I will copy and

00:54:40 --> 00:54:45
paste the solution into Anki so that I

00:54:42 --> 00:54:47
know that next time like I'm basically

00:54:44 --> 00:54:49
building up this Bank of stuff that I

00:54:46 --> 00:54:51
got wrong so that when it comes to

00:54:48 --> 00:54:52
cramming for the exam I can just kind of

00:54:50 --> 00:54:53
Blitz through all the stuff that I got

00:54:51 --> 00:54:55
wrong even that's probably slightly

00:54:52 --> 00:54:57
sub-optimal so the main word of warning

00:54:54 --> 00:54:59
is that be wary of making too many

00:54:56 --> 00:55:00
flashcards I think I'd recommend asking

00:54:58 --> 00:55:02
yourself do I really need a flashcard

00:54:59 --> 00:55:03
for this I've got a story that really

00:55:01 --> 00:55:05
kind of puts us into perspective and

00:55:02 --> 00:55:07
like once I made my video about Anki and

00:55:04 --> 00:55:08
about how I memorized essays with it I

00:55:06 --> 00:55:10
started getting loads of messages and

00:55:07 --> 00:55:11
emails from people saying that hey you

00:55:09 --> 00:55:12
know these are my decks can you have a

00:55:10 --> 00:55:13
look at it can you make sure I'm on the

00:55:11 --> 00:55:14
right track

00:55:12 --> 00:55:15
and there was one student tag that I

00:55:13 --> 00:55:18
found he was he was a medical student

00:55:14 --> 00:55:20
first year medical student and and he

00:55:17 --> 00:55:22
had like he had like sort of 8 000

00:55:19 --> 00:55:23
flashcards and immediately I was like

00:55:21 --> 00:55:24
okay this is way too many water what are

00:55:22 --> 00:55:25
you doing

00:55:23 --> 00:55:27
and then I looked through some of these

00:55:24 --> 00:55:29
and the one that stuck out was here he

00:55:26 --> 00:55:31
had a flashcard saying where is the

00:55:28 --> 00:55:32
art and the answer was in between the

00:55:30 --> 00:55:33
lungs

00:55:31 --> 00:55:35
you know where's the heart in between

00:55:33 --> 00:55:37
the lungs and that is one of those

00:55:34 --> 00:55:38
things that's just so obvious that you

00:55:36 --> 00:55:40
don't need a flash card for it it's also

00:55:37 --> 00:55:41
a bit pointless having a flash card

00:55:39 --> 00:55:43
because when you see the phrase where is

00:55:40 --> 00:55:45
the hot you know I would say okay fine

00:55:42 --> 00:55:46
if if you ask me where's the heart I

00:55:44 --> 00:55:48
would say all right well I suppose it's

00:55:45 --> 00:55:50
at the level of T5 vertebrae it's sort

00:55:47 --> 00:55:51
of in the middle media steinum it's

00:55:49 --> 00:55:52
urrounded by you know the anterior

00:55:50 --> 00:55:53
metastinum and the posterior

00:55:51 --> 00:55:54
metastatinum it's got the thoracic dog

00:55:52 --> 00:55:56
knee running alongside it's got the

00:55:53 --> 00:55:57
autocarch on top of it blah blah

00:55:55 --> 00:55:59
but if I look at the flashcard and the

00:55:57 --> 00:56:00
answer was in between the lungs I would

00:55:58 --> 00:56:01
feel cheated I would be like what the

00:55:59 --> 00:56:03
ll what is the point of this flash

00:56:00 --> 00:56:04
card this is a total waste of time so

00:56:02 --> 00:56:06
that's why I told him I was like look

00:56:03 --> 00:56:09
man you know avoid avoid making

00:56:05 --> 00:56:11
flashcards for obvious things and that's

00:56:08 --> 00:56:12
why I think that I wouldn't make I

00:56:10 --> 00:56:13
wouldn't personally make flash cards on

00:56:11 --> 00:56:16
my first pass through the content

00:56:12 --> 00:56:18
because who knows what the bits that

00:56:15 --> 00:56:19
I'll just know of by heart will will be

00:56:17 --> 00:56:20
and what the bits are going to be

00:56:18 --> 00:56:21
obvious and what bits aren't going to be

00:56:19 --> 00:56:23
obvious I would start making flashcards

00:56:21 --> 00:56:24
on my second or third pass through the

00:56:22 --> 00:56:26
content and only if I absolutely had to

00:56:23 --> 00:56:27
but that's all we say on flashcards for

00:56:25 --> 00:56:29
now

00:56:26 --> 00:56:31
all right so we've talked about how we

00:56:28 --> 00:56:34
might be able to use Anki as a flashcard

00:56:30 --> 00:56:35
software or Quizlet or you know do it on

00:56:33 --> 00:56:36
paper if you really want to and my

00:56:34 --> 00:56:39
friend Thomas Frank has a really good

00:56:35 --> 00:56:41
video on how to use paper flash cards

00:56:38 --> 00:56:43
and how to use a system to space

00:56:40 --> 00:56:45
repetition them uh if you want but to be

00:56:42 --> 00:56:48
honest he made that like four years ago

00:56:44 --> 00:56:50
uh before the flashcard app thing was

00:56:47 --> 00:56:52
like a really big deal so I'd fully

00:56:49 --> 00:56:54
recommend using an app for that but

00:56:51 --> 00:56:57
sometimes I don't like using flashcard

00:56:54 --> 00:57:00
apps like Anki I actually prefer to use

00:56:56 --> 00:57:02
Google Sheets uh just like a spreadsheet

00:56:59 --> 00:57:05
as a way like a sort of flashcard

00:57:01 --> 00:57:07
alternative especially if I'm not doing

00:57:04 --> 00:57:09
it like longitudinally over a very long

00:57:06 --> 00:57:10
time I think Anki is fantastic if you're

00:57:08 --> 00:57:12
able to put in the time to do it

00:57:09 --> 00:57:14
consistently over the long term but for

00:57:11 --> 00:57:16
example for my final year exams finally

00:57:13 --> 00:57:18
at OS Keys which are clinical exams

00:57:15 --> 00:57:20
where we have to

00:57:17 --> 00:57:21
um kind of talk to the examiner and

00:57:19 --> 00:57:23
basically show off on knowledge to The

00:57:20 --> 00:57:25
Examiner for that I only really started

00:57:22 --> 00:57:27
preparing about two months before the

00:57:24 --> 00:57:29
xam and I think that wasn't enough time

00:57:26 --> 00:57:31
to do everything properly in Anki so

00:57:28 --> 00:57:33
what I did instead was use Google Sheets

00:57:30 --> 00:57:34
as a sort of flashcard alternative and

00:57:32 --> 00:57:36
I've done a YouTube video exploring this

00:57:33 --> 00:57:39
but I'm going to put it in this video

00:57:35 --> 00:57:40
just for completeness because I think

00:57:38 --> 00:57:41
this is actually quite a good technique

00:57:39 --> 00:57:43
and I've had messages from loads of

00:57:40 --> 00:57:45
students saying that oh wow I've started

00:57:42 --> 00:57:47
using your Google Sheets technique as a

00:57:44 --> 00:57:48
flashcard alternative but equally I've

00:57:46 --> 00:57:49
had lots of messages from students

00:57:47 --> 00:57:51
aying oh I tried out your Google Sheets

00:57:48 --> 00:57:54
method but to be honest I prefer Anki

00:57:50 --> 00:57:55
and that's defined like as long as we're

00:57:53 --> 00:57:57
using active recall and space repetition

00:57:54 --> 00:57:58
in whatever study technique we're using

00:57:56 --> 00:58:01
it doesn't really matter if it's Google

00:57:57 --> 00:58:03
Sheets or if a tank here or whatever

00:58:00 --> 00:58:04
um so in a minute I will include the

00:58:02 --> 00:58:05
Google Sheets video here so you can have

00:58:03 --> 00:58:06
a look through that method if you

00:58:04 --> 00:58:08
haven't seen it yet but just a quick

00:58:06 --> 00:58:10
note I always use Google Sheets rather

00:58:07 --> 00:58:12
than Microsoft Excel because Google

00:58:09 --> 00:58:14
Sheets is free it's quick and it loads

00:58:11 --> 00:58:15
on any device whereas Microsoft Excel

00:58:13 --> 00:58:18
ike takes ages to open up it's a big

00:58:14 --> 00:58:20
heavy lumbering piece of software and I

00:58:17 --> 00:58:22
suppose now they've got support for

00:58:19 --> 00:58:23
different devices but I mean if I'm at

00:58:21 --> 00:58:24
work for example and I want to do some

00:58:22 --> 00:58:27
quick revision I can literally just go

00:58:24 --> 00:58:29
n Google Sheets on on the web whereas I

00:58:26 --> 00:58:31
can't open up Excel on the word

00:58:28 --> 00:58:32
computers and you know there's all sorts

00:58:30 --> 00:58:34
of reasons why I use Google Sheets

00:58:31 --> 00:58:35
rather than Microsoft Excel but yeah

00:58:33 --> 00:58:37
ere is the video where I will now talk

00:58:34 --> 00:58:39
about Google Sheets um I hope you enjoy

00:58:36 --> 00:58:40
it if you haven't seen it already I

00:58:38 --> 00:58:42
think you'll hopefully learn something

00:58:39 --> 00:58:43
from it thank you so we're going to jump

00:58:41 --> 00:58:45
in and I'm going to show you the Google

00:58:42 --> 00:58:48
sheet that I use to revise for my final

00:58:44 --> 00:58:49
year Medical School exams at Cambridge a

00:58:47 --> 00:58:51
few months ago and I'll just kind of

00:58:48 --> 00:58:52
explain how it works okay so here we

00:58:50 --> 00:58:54
have the Google sheet and you can see

00:58:51 --> 00:58:55
along the bottom I've got all the

00:58:53 --> 00:58:57
various different subjects so abdomen

00:58:54 --> 00:58:59
cardiovascular examination the

00:58:56 --> 00:59:03
respiratory examination neuro endocrine

00:58:58 --> 00:59:04
Durham owls thing it doesn't matter the

00:59:02 --> 00:59:06
point is the subject along the bottom

00:59:03 --> 00:59:09
and then the structure of the sheet is

00:59:05 --> 00:59:13
based on having questions in column A

00:59:08 --> 00:59:15
and answers in column B and it's pretty

00:59:12 --> 00:59:16
much that simple so let's look at my

00:59:14 --> 00:59:18
liver disease little category and I've

00:59:15 --> 00:59:20
got loads of questions to myself causes

00:59:17 --> 00:59:21
of hepatomegaly which means a big liver

00:59:19 --> 00:59:23
um what might be evidence of

00:59:20 --> 00:59:24
decompensation in liver disease what

00:59:22 --> 00:59:26
investigations would you like

00:59:23 --> 00:59:27
complications of cirrhosis

00:59:25 --> 00:59:29
classification of cirrhosis causes of

00:59:26 --> 00:59:31
ascites which means you know fluid in

00:59:28 --> 00:59:32
your abdomen uh how do we treat Society

00:59:30 --> 00:59:35
I mean all these various questions about

00:59:31 --> 00:59:39
liver disease and the cool thing is I've

00:59:34 --> 00:59:42
written the answers in column B but I

00:59:38 --> 00:59:44
have colored them in in font color white

00:59:41 --> 00:59:45
so I've wrote them obviously with font

00:59:43 --> 00:59:46
color black so I could see what I was

00:59:44 --> 00:59:48
doing but then once I've written the

00:59:45 --> 00:59:50
answer I

00:59:47 --> 00:59:52
know text color white which means when

00:59:49 --> 00:59:53
I'm looking through the spreadsheet I

00:59:51 --> 00:59:55
can't see any of the answers and

00:59:52 --> 00:59:57
therefore this is active recall when I

00:59:54 --> 00:59:59
see a question I have to actively

00:59:56 --> 01:00:01
retrieve the information from my brain

00:59:58 --> 01:00:02
which as we mentioned earlier and in all

01:00:00 --> 01:00:04
of my previous study videos is the

01:00:01 --> 01:00:05
single most efficient study technique so

01:00:03 --> 01:00:06
that's the whole point of this method

01:00:04 --> 01:00:08
it's just about having questions and

01:00:06 --> 01:00:09
answers and this is kind of like

01:00:07 --> 01:00:11
flashcards it's like having you know the

01:00:08 --> 01:00:12
front of a flash card as column A and

01:00:10 --> 01:00:14
the back of the flash card as column B

01:00:11 --> 01:00:16
but I just think this is a nicer way to

01:00:13 --> 01:00:18
look through a topic and we'll talk more

01:00:15 --> 01:00:19
about exactly why I like this method

01:00:17 --> 01:00:21
later on but that's pretty much the

01:00:18 --> 01:00:23
method questions in one column answers

01:00:21 --> 01:00:25
in the other column also you'll notice

01:00:22 --> 01:00:27
that I have color coded some of these so

01:00:24 --> 01:00:29
it's pretty straightforward green means

01:00:26 --> 01:00:30
I'm pretty sure I know the answer or I

01:00:28 --> 01:00:32
knew the answer six months ago when I

01:00:29 --> 01:00:34
was doing these exams

01:00:31 --> 01:00:36
um I don't think I can remember the

01:00:33 --> 01:00:38
answers to a lot of these but you know

01:00:35 --> 01:00:39
I'll do some space repetition and active

01:00:37 --> 01:00:41
recall of this particular spreadsheet

01:00:38 --> 01:00:43
later on yellow means I wasn't very good

01:00:40 --> 01:00:45
but I sort of half knew the answer and

01:00:42 --> 01:00:46
there's a few red ones down here what

01:00:44 --> 01:00:48
are some problems following

01:00:45 --> 01:00:50
transplantation that I could know the

01:00:48 --> 01:00:51
answer at all and sometimes I use orange

01:00:49 --> 01:00:53
for like you know a middle ground and

01:00:50 --> 01:00:55
towards the end of the revision period

01:00:52 --> 01:00:57
where it came to exams I was doing this

01:00:54 --> 01:00:59
thing where I would hide some columns

01:00:56 --> 01:01:00
that I didn't need like so between here

01:00:58 --> 01:01:02
and here yeah so there's quite a few

01:00:59 --> 01:01:03
green ones but I don't need to go

01:01:01 --> 01:01:06
through all the green ones so I can just

01:01:02 --> 01:01:08
hide these to

01:01:05 --> 01:01:10
um these two columns

01:01:07 --> 01:01:11
and then you end up cleaning up your

01:01:09 --> 01:01:13
spreadsheet over time and then you know

01:01:10 --> 01:01:14
if you do want to have a session or an

01:01:12 --> 01:01:15
hour or two where you're just going

01:01:13 --> 01:01:18
through every single question you can

01:01:14 --> 01:01:20
just bash through them and this is the

01:01:17 --> 01:01:21
Google Sheets method for active recall I

01:01:19 --> 01:01:23
hope you enjoyed it now let's talk about

01:01:21 --> 01:01:25
exactly why I use this instead of

01:01:22 --> 01:01:27
lashcards

01:01:24 --> 01:01:29
okay so why do I use Google Sheets

01:01:26 --> 01:01:31
instead of flashcards uh there's a few

01:01:28 --> 01:01:33
reasons firstly I really like the fact

01:01:30 --> 01:01:35
hat putting it in a spreadsheet format

01:01:32 --> 01:01:37
gives you a systematic structure of

01:01:34 --> 01:01:40
reviewing your stuff flashcards are good

01:01:36 --> 01:01:42
because they tend to randomize the order

01:01:39 --> 01:01:43
in which questions are asked which is

01:01:41 --> 01:01:45
good because it means that you're having

01:01:42 --> 01:01:47
to dredge up information that's in

01:01:44 --> 01:01:48
different topics and there is this thing

01:01:46 --> 01:01:50
in efficient studying that you know

01:01:47 --> 01:01:52
interleaved practice that the more you

01:01:49 --> 01:01:55
learn from different categories the more

01:01:51 --> 01:01:56
likely you are to remember it but I'm

01:01:54 --> 01:01:58
not a massive fan of that especially

01:01:55 --> 01:02:00
within medicine and a lot of other

01:01:57 --> 01:02:01
subjects where the sort of recall you're

01:01:59 --> 01:02:03
going to be asked to do is going to be

01:02:00 --> 01:02:05
systematic like let's say I see a

01:02:02 --> 01:02:06
patient who has a stoma in their abdomen

01:02:04 --> 01:02:08
I'm going to be asked questions about

01:02:05 --> 01:02:09
he stoma they might ask me what the

01:02:07 --> 01:02:11
complications of stomas are like the

01:02:08 --> 01:02:13
question over here they might ask me

01:02:10 --> 01:02:14
about different indications for a stoma

01:02:12 --> 01:02:16
they might ask me what a Hartman's

01:02:13 --> 01:02:18
procedure is there's all stuff related

01:02:15 --> 01:02:21
to a stoma whereas if I had these in an

01:02:17 --> 01:02:21
Anki or Quizlet deck then I might get a

01:02:20 --> 01:02:23
question about what are the

01:02:20 --> 01:02:25
complications of stoma and then the next

01:02:22 --> 01:02:26
question would be how do patients with

01:02:24 --> 01:02:28
all to Thermal dominant polycytic kidney

01:02:25 --> 01:02:29
disease present and the next question

01:02:27 --> 01:02:31
might be what are the complications

01:02:28 --> 01:02:33
following a liver transplant I mean it's

01:02:30 --> 01:02:34
good to have this knowledge off the

01:02:32 --> 01:02:37
top of your head but I think I prefer

01:02:33 --> 01:02:39
the systematic approach where I can have

01:02:36 --> 01:02:41
you know this mental model of stomas and

01:02:38 --> 01:02:43
just kind of know everything about them

01:02:40 --> 01:02:45
in one place and actively revise that

01:02:42 --> 01:02:47
one Topic at a time rather than just

01:02:44 --> 01:02:48
getting a scatter gun of different

01:02:46 --> 01:02:50
questions about various problems in the

01:02:47 --> 01:02:52
abdomen secondly I really like how this

01:02:49 --> 01:02:54
lets me see at a glance exactly where

01:02:51 --> 01:02:56
the holes in my knowledge are with

01:02:53 --> 01:02:58
something like Anki inquisite I have to

01:02:55 --> 01:03:00
trust that their space repetition

01:02:57 --> 01:03:01
algorithm is going to flag up the things

01:02:59 --> 01:03:03
that I don't know and often I'll have to

01:03:00 --> 01:03:05
go through a hundred cards where I'm

01:03:02 --> 01:03:06
pretty sure I know like 80 of them and

01:03:04 --> 01:03:08
it's only 20 of them that I'm really

01:03:05 --> 01:03:10
struggling with whereas in this I have

01:03:07 --> 01:03:12
color coded in myself stuff that I'm put

01:03:09 --> 01:03:13
in red means that last time I tested

01:03:11 --> 01:03:15
myself on this I didn't know how to do

01:03:12 --> 01:03:17
it so that's the stuff I'm going to do

01:03:14 --> 01:03:18
first and if I just scroll through the

01:03:16 --> 01:03:20
ABDO exam I see there's quite a few red

01:03:17 --> 01:03:22
yellows and oranges so I'm going to

01:03:19 --> 01:03:24
spend some time on this today and I

01:03:21 --> 01:03:26
don't think oh yeah cardio exam there's

01:03:23 --> 01:03:28
more green green and yellow here there's

01:03:25 --> 01:03:30
a few Reds so I'd be looking at these

01:03:27 --> 01:03:32
Reds and this becomes a very quick way

01:03:29 --> 01:03:33
of reviewing questions I'm not a fan of

01:03:31 --> 01:03:35
reviewing notes obviously because that's

01:03:32 --> 01:03:36
not efficient that's passive it's just

01:03:34 --> 01:03:38
reading information I'm a big fan of

01:03:35 --> 01:03:39
active recall and this makes it super

01:03:37 --> 01:03:40
easy to see where the holes in my

01:03:38 --> 01:03:42
knowledge are thirdly I think the Google

01:03:39 --> 01:03:44
Sheets method makes it slightly easier

01:03:41 --> 01:03:45
to work together with friends so if

01:03:43 --> 01:03:46
you've seen any of my previous study

01:03:44 --> 01:03:48
with me videos from a few months ago

01:03:45 --> 01:03:49
when my friends and I were preparing for

01:03:47 --> 01:03:51
our med school exams we'd often have

01:03:48 --> 01:03:54
like sessions where I'd put up the

01:03:50 --> 01:03:56
Google sheet on my big monitor Zoom the

01:03:53 --> 01:03:58
font size in and we'd kind of sit there

01:03:55 --> 01:04:00
and work through the questions one at a

01:03:57 --> 01:04:01
time that is possible with flashcards I

01:03:59 --> 01:04:03
have done it in the past with flashcards

01:04:00 --> 01:04:05
but I just found that having tried both

01:04:02 --> 01:04:06
of them it was It was kind of nicer to

01:04:04 --> 01:04:07
just go through a spreadsheet and be

01:04:05 --> 01:04:10
like one by one next next next next

01:04:06 --> 01:04:11
rather than trust that random flashcards

01:04:09 --> 01:04:12
would pop up in the right order and

01:04:10 --> 01:04:14
finally the reason I prefer Google

01:04:11 --> 01:04:16
Sheets to flashcards is because

01:04:13 --> 01:04:17
flashcards become an absolute burden

01:04:15 --> 01:04:19
like I mentioned at the start of the

01:04:16 --> 01:04:21
video you see that you've got 150

01:04:18 --> 01:04:22
flashcards to review that day and it

01:04:20 --> 01:04:24
just sucks all the enjoyment out of

01:04:21 --> 01:04:26
learning and yeah I know you can set

01:04:23 --> 01:04:27
hem so you review 20 at a time but then

01:04:25 --> 01:04:28
sometimes you want to change that amount

01:04:26 --> 01:04:29
and sometimes you want to work more and

01:04:27 --> 01:04:30
then you have to actively go into the

01:04:28 --> 01:04:32
options and change that blah blah all

01:04:29 --> 01:04:34
this stuff essentially Google Sheets

01:04:31 --> 01:04:37
just makes it a super low commitment

01:04:33 --> 01:04:39
hing to sit down and study even if I

01:04:36 --> 01:04:41
have five or ten minutes I can just go

01:04:38 --> 01:04:43
pen Google Sheets find the things that

01:04:40 --> 01:04:44
are read and actively try and recall

01:04:42 --> 01:04:46
them and then look up the answers or

01:04:43 --> 01:04:47
Google or Wikipedia or look at a

01:04:45 --> 01:04:50
textbook for the stuff that I don't

01:04:46 --> 01:04:51
remember and that you know even if I

01:04:49 --> 01:04:53
have five or ten minutes that will work

01:04:50 --> 01:04:54
whereas for a flash card yeah it does

01:04:52 --> 01:04:56
work if you just have a few minutes at a

01:04:54 --> 01:04:58
time but I often found when I was using

01:04:55 --> 01:05:00
flashcards intensively in first and

01:04:57 --> 01:05:02
second year that unless I had like an

01:05:00 --> 01:05:04
hour or two to sit down and do my flash

01:05:01 --> 01:05:06
cards I used to treat it as such a big

01:05:03 --> 01:05:08
deal that I ended up not reviewing them

01:05:06 --> 01:05:09
very often so I like the fact that

01:05:07 --> 01:05:11
Google Sheets is low commitment you can

01:05:08 --> 01:05:13
do it as little or as much as you want

01:05:10 --> 01:05:14
hat's kind of nice it's kind of nice

01:05:12 --> 01:05:15
going through them and there's that

01:05:13 --> 01:05:18
little gamification element of it as

01:05:14 --> 01:05:19
well where once once you've recall

01:05:17 --> 01:05:20
something well then you mock it as

01:05:18 --> 01:05:22
yellow you mock it as green and it gives

01:05:19 --> 01:05:23
you that little burst of endorphins that

01:05:21 --> 01:05:25
make you feel good about studying

01:05:22 --> 01:05:27
overall so yeah if I have some spare

01:05:24 --> 01:05:28
time and I've got exams coming up I just

01:05:26 --> 01:05:31
bash open Google Sheets on my iPhone or

01:05:27 --> 01:05:33
iPad or laptop or even a random browser

01:05:30 --> 01:05:34
and it just works it's just pretty

01:05:32 --> 01:05:36
magical

01:05:33 --> 01:05:38
finally I just want to end by addressing

01:05:35 --> 01:05:39
a question that I've had from loads of

01:05:37 --> 01:05:41
people over the last year or so that

01:05:38 --> 01:05:43
I've also mentioned this Google Sheets

01:05:40 --> 01:05:44
thing and people always ask what do you

01:05:42 --> 01:05:46
make a flash card for and what do you

01:05:43 --> 01:05:49
se your Google sheet for and I really

01:05:45 --> 01:05:50
don't have that distinct categorization

01:05:48 --> 01:05:53
in general if I was to come up with a

01:05:49 --> 01:05:55
rule of thumb I prefer flashcards for

01:05:52 --> 01:05:58
very very isolated facts so for example

01:05:54 --> 01:06:00
what is the dose of co-amoxiclav for I

01:05:57 --> 01:06:01
don't know a community called pneumonia

01:05:59 --> 01:06:03
nd it would be about 625 milligrams

01:06:00 --> 01:06:04
four times a day or three times a day or

01:06:02 --> 01:06:07
whatever you know it's an isolated fact

01:06:03 --> 01:06:09
what type of virus is a flaby virus it's

01:06:06 --> 01:06:11
a double-stranded DNA RNA virus that

01:06:08 --> 01:06:13
does blah blah you know discrete

01:06:10 --> 01:06:15
isolated facts I kind of prefer Google

01:06:12 --> 01:06:17
Sheets for more General understanding

01:06:14 --> 01:06:18
concepts I said in one of my previous

01:06:16 --> 01:06:21
videos that I quite like the phrase

01:06:17 --> 01:06:23
what's the deal with polymerica it's a

01:06:20 --> 01:06:23
bad question to put on a flashcard

01:06:22 --> 01:06:25
because if you look at the evidence

01:06:22 --> 01:06:28
running flashcards people always say

01:06:24 --> 01:06:30
that it's better to have you know a

01:06:27 --> 01:06:32
distinct fact whereas just explain

01:06:29 --> 01:06:34
polymerica is a very broad open-ended

01:06:31 --> 01:06:35
question I think that's the sort of

01:06:33 --> 01:06:37
thing that I like putting in my Google

01:06:34 --> 01:06:38
sheet because if I don't know what it is

01:06:36 --> 01:06:40
I'll just Google it and I'm on the

01:06:37 --> 01:06:41
computer anyway because I'm going from a

01:06:39 --> 01:06:42
Google sheet so I'll just Google it find

01:06:40 --> 01:06:44
out what it is and then you know either

01:06:41 --> 01:06:46
write some of it down on my sheet on my

01:06:43 --> 01:06:47
sheet or just make a mental note in my

01:06:45 --> 01:06:49
head to repeat that information to

01:06:46 --> 01:06:51
rehearse it to active recall it and then

01:06:48 --> 01:06:53
I can mark that particular box as yellow

01:06:50 --> 01:06:54
another question people often ask is do

01:06:52 --> 01:06:56
you write out your whole answers in full

01:06:54 --> 01:06:59
in the flash card or in the Google sheet

01:06:55 --> 01:07:00
and the answer is sometimes if it's a

01:06:58 --> 01:07:02
simple if it's a simple thing like

01:06:59 --> 01:07:03
what's the differential for aortic

01:07:01 --> 01:07:05
stenosis I.E an injection systolic

01:07:03 --> 01:07:08
murmur there's just four points here

01:07:04 --> 01:07:10
hocm vsd aortic Sclerosis

01:07:07 --> 01:07:13
at least at the medical student final

01:07:09 --> 01:07:14
evel only four things I can write four

01:07:12 --> 01:07:17
things in down in my little spreadsheet

01:07:13 --> 01:07:19
column but if the question was explain

01:07:16 --> 01:07:21
polymerica I probably wouldn't write

01:07:18 --> 01:07:23
down anything in the column because I

01:07:20 --> 01:07:25
would trust that if I don't know what it

01:07:22 --> 01:07:27
is if I can't explain it to myself in my

01:07:24 --> 01:07:28
head I'll just Google it and then I'll

01:07:26 --> 01:07:30
be able to explain it so a lot of the

01:07:27 --> 01:07:32
time I think we can waste a lot of time

01:07:29 --> 01:07:34
making the flashcards but especially for

01:07:31 --> 01:07:36
stuff like medicine where every single

01:07:33 --> 01:07:38
thing is just to Google away there's

01:07:35 --> 01:07:39
almost no point in adding tons of

01:07:37 --> 01:07:41
information and duplicating that

01:07:38 --> 01:07:42
information and making your own notes

01:07:40 --> 01:07:43
because you can just Google it if you

01:07:41 --> 01:07:45
don't know what the answer is so yeah

01:07:42 --> 01:07:46
opefully that clarifies those two

01:07:44 --> 01:07:48
points

01:07:45 --> 01:07:50
um I don't really have that distinctive

01:07:47 --> 01:07:51
categorization between flash cards and

01:07:49 --> 01:07:53
sheets but I kind of prefer Google

01:07:50 --> 01:07:55
Sheets for more general concepts and

01:07:52 --> 01:07:57
flashcards for more distinct facts even

01:07:54 --> 01:07:58
though there's a lot of overlap and

01:07:56 --> 01:08:00
secondly I sometimes don't write out the

01:07:57 --> 01:08:02
answers in the column at all if I can

01:07:59 --> 01:08:03
just Google it so yeah I hope you found

01:08:01 --> 01:08:05
this video useful we've talked about why

01:08:02 --> 01:08:07
I use Google Sheets we've talked about

01:08:04 --> 01:08:09
how I use Google Sheets it's pretty much

01:08:06 --> 01:08:10
the same as flashcards but I think the

01:08:08 --> 01:08:12
Google sheet method addresses some of

01:08:09 --> 01:08:13
the issues that I personally have found

01:08:11 --> 01:08:15
with flashcards and that's a point that

01:08:12 --> 01:08:17
I want to end on this is just what works

01:08:15 --> 01:08:19
for me this thing about Google Sheets

01:08:16 --> 01:08:21
being better than flashcards is

01:08:18 --> 01:08:23
obviously not gospel truth the only

01:08:20 --> 01:08:25
gospel truth in this whole study tips

01:08:22 --> 01:08:26
tuff is active recall and space

01:08:24 --> 01:08:28
repetition in fact it's better than

01:08:25 --> 01:08:29
gospel truth because there is a mountain

01:08:27 --> 01:08:32
of evidence supporting it controversial

01:08:28 --> 01:08:33
active recall space repetition things

01:08:31 --> 01:08:35
also things like elaborative encoding

01:08:32 --> 01:08:36
and interleague practice you can find

01:08:34 --> 01:08:38
out more if you read the book make it

01:08:35 --> 01:08:40
stick but I'll be working on videos over

01:08:37 --> 01:08:41
those on those over time

01:08:39 --> 01:08:43
um but yeah this is just like a personal

01:08:40 --> 01:08:44
preference thing I'm making this video

01:08:42 --> 01:08:46
because a lot of people have asked about

01:08:43 --> 01:08:48
it I prefer Google Sheets to flashcards

01:08:45 --> 01:08:50
for some things that's not to say it

01:08:47 --> 01:08:51
works for everyone alright so that was

01:08:49 --> 01:08:54
the Google Sheets method as a flash card

01:08:50 --> 01:08:56
as an Anki alternative

01:08:53 --> 01:08:58
all right so we are continuing our Saga

01:08:55 --> 01:09:00
of effective techniques for remembering

01:08:57 --> 01:09:02
stuff having of course established that

01:08:59 --> 01:09:04
step one is to understand and only step

01:09:01 --> 01:09:05
two is to remember and in this video I

01:09:03 --> 01:09:08
want to talk about mind maps or spider

01:09:04 --> 01:09:09
diagrams now the basic idea is that you

01:09:07 --> 01:09:11
have a concept in the middle of your

01:09:08 --> 01:09:14
page and then you have like the the

01:09:10 --> 01:09:15
branches of that concept sort of going

01:09:13 --> 01:09:17
out from the middle then you have the

01:09:14 --> 01:09:19
subcategories of that branches going out

01:09:16 --> 01:09:20
from that and I quite like mind maps and

01:09:18 --> 01:09:23
the reason I like them is because they

01:09:19 --> 01:09:26
help me get a broad understanding of

01:09:22 --> 01:09:28
what the content that I'm looking at is

01:09:25 --> 01:09:30
and so here is for example what a mind

01:09:27 --> 01:09:31
map looks like so hopefully you can see

01:09:29 --> 01:09:33
on the page this was something that I

01:09:30 --> 01:09:34
wrote uh on a piece of paper and then I

01:09:32 --> 01:09:36
scanned it into OneNote because I was

01:09:33 --> 01:09:38
using OneNote at the time before notion

01:09:35 --> 01:09:40
was a thing and so you can see in the

01:09:37 --> 01:09:42
middle we've got bleeding and clotting

01:09:39 --> 01:09:44
as sort of one of the strands of

01:09:41 --> 01:09:46
hematology which is the study of blood

01:09:43 --> 01:09:48
and I've split that up into prokaryate

01:09:45 --> 01:09:50
so clotting disorders and anticoagulant

01:09:47 --> 01:09:51
so bleeding disorders so things that

01:09:49 --> 01:09:53
make you clot and things that make you

01:09:50 --> 01:09:55
bleed and we can see the program is

01:09:52 --> 01:09:56
plit up into her military and Acquired

01:09:54 --> 01:09:59
and then we've got a few different

01:09:55 --> 01:10:00
hings within Acquired and we've got a

01:09:58 --> 01:10:02
few different things in hereditary and

01:09:59 --> 01:10:04
then anticoagulant is calling Factor the

01:10:01 --> 01:10:06
blood vessels platelets platelet number

01:10:03 --> 01:10:08
plate and and so you can see that

01:10:05 --> 01:10:10
hopefully that this gives me an idea of

01:10:07 --> 01:10:12
what all the different categories are

01:10:09 --> 01:10:14
and kind of like in in the video earlier

01:10:11 --> 01:10:16
on if you saw that about scoping the

01:10:13 --> 01:10:18
subject a mind map is sort of like where

01:10:15 --> 01:10:19
you start with the base of the tree and

01:10:17 --> 01:10:21
then you Branch out into different

01:10:18 --> 01:10:23
hings then you can put the leaves and

01:10:20 --> 01:10:24
flowers and stuff on the branches I

01:10:22 --> 01:10:26
don't know how far this metaphor extends

01:10:23 --> 01:10:28
but so for example if we're talking

01:10:25 --> 01:10:30
about protein C deficiency or protein s

01:10:27 --> 01:10:32
deficiency I know that they are

01:10:29 --> 01:10:34
deficiencies I know that they are

01:10:31 --> 01:10:35
hereditary I know they're pro-coagulant

01:10:33 --> 01:10:37
so they make you clot and I know they're

01:10:34 --> 01:10:39
sort of within this branch of clotting

01:10:36 --> 01:10:40
hematology disorders and so this is sort

01:10:38 --> 01:10:41
of what a mind map looks like and I

01:10:39 --> 01:10:43
think this is great because otherwise

01:10:40 --> 01:10:44
like you know I'll use the example of

01:10:42 --> 01:10:47
med school but apply this to whatever

01:10:43 --> 01:10:50
subject you're doing otherwise you know

01:10:46 --> 01:10:52
if we if we looked at the the textbook

01:10:49 --> 01:10:53
and we looked at kind of bleeding and

01:10:51 --> 01:10:55
clotting disorders in the Oxford

01:10:52 --> 01:10:56
Handbook of clinical medicine thing we

01:10:54 --> 01:10:58
would just have a long list and I think

01:10:55 --> 01:10:59
having it in this sort of Mind map

01:10:57 --> 01:11:01
format makes it really obvious at least

01:10:58 --> 01:11:03
for me in my head like where the thing

01:11:00 --> 01:11:05
fits into the bigger picture and this is

01:11:02 --> 01:11:06
omething that I talk about a lot that I

01:11:04 --> 01:11:08
think is really helpful to keep in mind

01:11:05 --> 01:11:11
that no one really explicitly tells us

01:11:07 --> 01:11:12
that whenever we are learning anything

01:11:10 --> 01:11:14
we want to make sure we want to First

01:11:11 --> 01:11:16
understand where it fits into the bigger

01:11:13 --> 01:11:18
picture which sort of goes hand in hand

01:11:15 --> 01:11:20
with scoping the subject so this is one

01:11:17 --> 01:11:22
xample of a mind map so this is my mind

01:11:19 --> 01:11:23
amount for Pediatrics I've just

01:11:21 --> 01:11:26
basically put the whole syllabus on one

01:11:22 --> 01:11:28
single page so that if I am revising

01:11:29 --> 01:11:34
be like okay do I know do I know

01:11:31 --> 01:11:37
everything about bronchiolitis asthma

01:11:33 --> 01:11:39
viral wheeze uh croup and epiglottitis

01:11:36 --> 01:11:40
CF cystic fibrosis and pneumonia okay

01:11:38 --> 01:11:42
cool that's fine I'm done with

01:11:39 --> 01:11:44
respiratory let me look at gastro right

01:11:41 --> 01:11:45
do I know about inflammatory bowel

01:11:43 --> 01:11:46
disease do I know about functional

01:11:44 --> 01:11:48
abdominal pain do I know about

01:11:45 --> 01:11:50
necrotizing enterocolitis do I know

01:11:47 --> 01:11:52
about hush prank disease do I know about

01:11:49 --> 01:11:54
gourd gastroesophageal reflux disease do

01:11:51 --> 01:11:55
I know about hus

01:11:53 --> 01:11:57
about gastroenteritis caused by fluids

01:11:54 --> 01:11:59
and dehydrated now I know about pyoric

01:11:56 --> 01:12:00
stenosis do I know about intussusception

01:11:58 --> 01:12:02
do I know about Scenic disease these are

01:11:59 --> 01:12:04
all the gastro-related things within

01:12:01 --> 01:12:05
Pediatrics so firstly what I can do is I

can look at the Mind map and then try

and active recall everything I know

01:12:06 --> 01:12:09
about these things and then I can

and look it up so that was one way of

what I can do is I can turn I can turn

from scratch so it could be like right

Pediatrics right we're going to look at

a little bit of respiratory what are all

the Pediatric respiratory conditions

that I know of and I will sit there and

I know that cystic fibrosis is a thing I

know that asthma bronchiolitis croup

and I'll be like okay I can't remember

hat eight years after I first learned

01:18:46 --> 01:18:50

01:18:48 --> 01:18:52
hat I want to learn these techniques

01:18:52 --> 01:18:57

01:18:57 --> 01:19:01
of system to memory I ended up not

01:18:58 --> 01:19:02
really using it in my first year but it

01:19:00 --> 01:19:03
ended up coming pretty handy in my

01:19:01 --> 01:19:06
second and third year the PEC system

01:19:03 --> 01:19:09
it's based on the concept that we like

01:19:05 --> 01:19:11
as humans we have a very poor memory for

01:19:08 --> 01:19:13
numbers and for words but we have a very

01:19:10 --> 01:19:15
good memory for Concepts like we can

01:19:12 --> 01:19:16
imagine a chair in our minds but it'll

01:19:14 --> 01:19:18
be a lot harder to remember the number

01:19:15 --> 01:19:21
unning at 55 or 1984 or whatever

01:19:17 --> 01:19:23
because we don't have like numbers and

01:19:20 --> 01:19:24
words are like abstract things and our

01:19:22 --> 01:19:26
brain is not very good at remembering

01:19:23 --> 01:19:28
abstract things whereas if you can

01:19:25 --> 01:19:29
visualize something then it's a lot

01:19:27 --> 01:19:31
better at remembering that so the idea

01:19:28 --> 01:19:34
behind the pixel is that you want to

01:19:30 --> 01:19:36
convert numbers into words and then you

01:19:33 --> 01:19:38
can visualize the words this is Harry

01:19:35 --> 01:19:40
Lorraine's kind of Peg system for memory

01:19:37 --> 01:19:41
and I think in Darren Brown's book a

01:19:39 --> 01:19:43
trick of the mind he's also got another

01:19:40 --> 01:19:45
Peg system but this is just one of them

01:19:42 --> 01:19:47
so here's how it works so the sound for

01:19:44 --> 01:19:49
one will always be a t or a d and you

01:19:46 --> 01:19:50
imagine that a t has one downstroke the

01:19:48 --> 01:19:53
sound for two will always be an N

01:19:49 --> 01:19:56
because if you imagine like an N has two

01:19:52 --> 01:19:57
downstrokes three is M because M has

01:19:55 --> 01:20:00
three downstrokes with it

01:19:56 --> 01:20:01
wo one two three

01:19:59 --> 01:20:02
um the sound for four will always be R

01:20:00 --> 01:20:06
which is the final sign of the word four

01:20:01 --> 01:20:09
so four is r five is L which is a Roman

01:20:05 --> 01:20:12
umeral for fifty six is

01:20:08 --> 01:20:14
ETC so you know that that sort of sound

01:20:11 --> 01:20:16
um which is sort of like the letter J

01:20:13 --> 01:20:18
turned around sign for seven will be a k

01:20:15 --> 01:20:19
the sign for eight will be F of V

01:20:17 --> 01:20:22
someone will be P or B it's not for zero

01:20:18 --> 01:20:24
will be S or Z four zero

01:20:21 --> 01:20:26
so that means if we need the number one

01:20:23 --> 01:20:29
we're seeing we're thinking of the sound

01:20:25 --> 01:20:31
the number number two number two would

01:20:28 --> 01:20:33
be the sound number three Ma and so if

01:20:30 --> 01:20:35
we want to turn those into images One

01:20:32 --> 01:20:38
becomes Thai because the only sort of

01:20:34 --> 01:20:41
consonant sound in Thai is just a ter so

01:20:37 --> 01:20:43
we know tie is one nine is B because the

01:20:40 --> 01:20:45
only consonant sound in in nine is in B

01:20:42 --> 01:20:47
is a b and we know bird is nine but if

01:20:44 --> 01:20:49
we're combining numbers together let's

01:20:46 --> 01:20:52
ay we're doing 54 that's the word lure

01:20:48 --> 01:20:57
because we know five is L and four is R

01:20:51 --> 01:20:58
so Le lier lior so I would probably

01:20:56 --> 01:21:01
convert I would I would be thinking

01:20:57 --> 01:21:02
either Leo or liar or a liar as in the

01:21:00 --> 01:21:04
type of music musical instrument the

01:21:01 --> 01:21:06
point is it's got two consonant sounds

01:21:03 --> 01:21:08
the Le and there are so I know that when

01:21:05 --> 01:21:11
I think of lure or liar I'm thinking of

01:21:07 --> 01:21:13
the number 54. equally you know 76 we

01:21:10 --> 01:21:15
know that seven has a k sound based on

01:21:12 --> 01:21:17
this thing and the way I remember is

01:21:14 --> 01:21:19
that seven sort of looks like a k sort

01:21:16 --> 01:21:22
of and if we look at the number 76 we

01:21:18 --> 01:21:24
know that six is the soft J or J or sure

01:21:21 --> 01:21:26
sound based on what we've memorized with

01:21:23 --> 01:21:29
our Peg system so we know that cage will

01:21:25 --> 01:21:32
be Kirch which is 76 and so if we

01:21:28 --> 01:21:34
imagine an image of a cage we know that

01:21:31 --> 01:21:35
must correspond to the number 76.

01:21:33 --> 01:21:38
so this this takes a little bit of

01:21:34 --> 01:21:39
effort right so if if you want to use

01:21:37 --> 01:21:40
this properly you want to get it to a

01:21:38 --> 01:21:43
point where it's completely second

01:21:39 --> 01:21:44
nature and I'm quite Rusty in this

because I haven't had the need to

01:21:43 --> 01:21:48

01:21:45 --> 01:21:51

o I would have thought thought of

and I hope you enjoy it if you haven't

01:27:09 --> 01:27:13
seen it already so there are basically

01:27:10 --> 01:27:15
two stages to this method the first

01:27:12 --> 01:27:16
age is the creation stage and the

01:27:14 --> 01:27:18
second stage is the memorization stage

01:27:15 --> 01:27:21
so in the creation stage the objective

01:27:17 --> 01:27:22
is to create First Class essay plans for

01:27:20 --> 01:27:24
every conceivable essay title that they

01:27:21 --> 01:27:25
could throw at us in the exam and in the

01:27:23 --> 01:27:27
memorization stage we're going to be

01:27:24 --> 01:27:29
committing all of these essay plans to

01:27:26 --> 01:27:31
memory by systematically using active

01:27:28 --> 01:27:33
recall space repetition spider diagrams

01:27:30 --> 01:27:34
and flashcards the idea is that by the

01:27:32 --> 01:27:37
time the exam rolls around you'll have

01:27:33 --> 01:27:38
memorized so many essay plans that a lot

01:27:36 --> 01:27:39
of them will just come up in the exam

01:27:37 --> 01:27:40
anyway because you've predicted the

01:27:38 --> 01:27:42
titles and you'll just be able to

01:27:39 --> 01:27:44
regurgitate stuff from your brain onto

01:27:41 --> 01:27:45
the paper but even if stuff comes up

01:27:43 --> 01:27:47
that you haven't memorized you'll know

01:27:44 --> 01:27:48
so much about the subject and you'll

01:27:46 --> 01:27:50
have so many content blocks in your head

01:27:47 --> 01:27:52
that you'll be able to generate a First

01:27:49 --> 01:27:53
Class essay from scratch so that was a

01:27:51 --> 01:27:55
general overview let's now talk about

01:27:52 --> 01:27:58
he two components the creation stage

01:27:54 --> 01:27:59
and the memorization stage in turn

01:27:57 --> 01:28:01
so the broad objective of the creation

01:27:58 --> 01:28:03
stage is to create a large number of

01:28:00 --> 01:28:04
really really good essay plans that you

01:28:02 --> 01:28:06
can then memorize in the memorization

01:28:03 --> 01:28:08
stage and regurgitate onto paper during

01:28:05 --> 01:28:09
your exam now it's probably beyond the

01:28:10 --> 01:28:15

01:28:12 --> 01:28:16

01:28:14 --> 01:28:18
questions and that's firstly how you

01:28:15 --> 01:28:20
decide what essay titles to pick

01:28:17 --> 01:28:21
secondly how you plan the essay and

01:28:19 --> 01:28:23
thirdly how you make sure your essay

01:28:20 --> 01:28:25
plan is really really good so let's deal

01:28:22 --> 01:28:26
with those in turn so firstly how do we

01:28:24 --> 01:28:28
decide what essays we're going to

01:28:25 --> 01:28:31
prepare the objective here is to scope

01:28:27 --> 01:28:32
the subject and find essay titles that

01:28:30 --> 01:28:34
cover the entire breadth of the syllabus

01:28:31 --> 01:28:36
now the easiest way to do this is to

01:28:33 --> 01:28:37
look at past papers and look at whatever

01:28:35 --> 01:28:39
past papers you have available and see

01:28:36 --> 01:28:41
what essays have come up in the past and

01:28:38 --> 01:28:42
you start off with those and then once

01:28:40 --> 01:28:44
you've planned out those essays you'll

01:28:42 --> 01:28:45
know enough about that subject in

01:28:43 --> 01:28:47
particular that you'll be able to put

01:28:44 --> 01:28:49
yourself in the shoes of examiners and

01:28:46 --> 01:28:51

01:29:06 --> 01:29:10
eed to actually plan those essays and

this is the fun part this is the part

hat actually requires doing some doing

01:29:11 --> 01:29:16
some cognitive labor so the way I would

01:29:13 --> 01:29:18
o this is that I'd give myself one day

of uni I was a slacker I only made like

hat particular question I would ignore

so this is my 10 page document about

heory of mind I've copied and pasted

he bottom to where I got the

01:30:40 --> 01:30:44
information from so if I need to return

to it I'll be able to find it again and

I spend the next few hours planning out

01:30:48 --> 01:30:53
he essay and actually writing it out

01:30:49 --> 01:30:54
properly so here is my plan is theory of

Mind a useful concept for understanding

from books from the review papers from

the lecture nodes from Google and I've

that I'm ultimately going to memorize

and as you can see over here I've pretty

much done this for everything within my

01:31:15 --> 01:31:19

01:31:16 --> 01:31:20
cognition which is all about the

01:31:18 --> 01:31:23
thinking of animals can animals plan for

01:31:19 --> 01:31:24
the future causality cognitive Maps the

convergent evolution theory of

intelligence do animals have a theory of

mind is a theorem unusual concept and

01:31:26 --> 01:31:30
you can see here I've written Anki

01:31:28 --> 01:31:32
beside them which is a foreshadowing as

to what's going to come later in this

01:31:31 --> 01:31:35
video so now we've done a research

document we've planned this essay we've

01:31:34 --> 01:31:38
pretty much written it out based on our

01:31:36 --> 01:31:40
esearch document and we only given

01:31:37 --> 01:31:42
ourselves one day to do this because of

01:31:39 --> 01:31:43
Parkinson's law that work expands to

01:31:41 --> 01:31:45
fill the time we allocate to it but how

01:31:42 --> 01:31:47
do we make that essay plan actually good

01:31:45 --> 01:31:49
um a lot of things go into good essay

01:31:46 --> 01:31:51
plan but in my opinion there are three

01:31:48 --> 01:31:52
things that count number one structure

01:31:50 --> 01:31:54
number two actually answering the

01:31:51 --> 01:31:56
question and number three having a bit

01:31:53 --> 01:31:58
of flair a bit of a little spice that

01:31:55 --> 01:31:59
you're sprinkling in your essay plan and

01:31:57 --> 01:32:00
I think the introduction is the most

01:31:58 --> 01:32:02
important part of the essay because in

01:32:00 --> 01:32:04
the introduction you can signal to the

01:32:01 --> 01:32:06
xaminer that you're doing all three of

01:32:03 --> 01:32:07
these things and when the examiner's

01:32:05 --> 01:32:08
marking your paper they're probably

01:32:06 --> 01:32:10
really bored they've read hundreds of

01:32:07 --> 01:32:11
these scripts already you want to hit

01:32:09 --> 01:32:13
hem with like a really legit

01:32:10 --> 01:32:14
introduction so here's an example of an

01:32:12 --> 01:32:16
introduction from one of my essays about

01:32:13 --> 01:32:19
whether judgment and decision making is

01:32:15 --> 01:32:20
cognitive or illogical or affective I.E

01:32:18 --> 01:32:22
emotional so I've written that the

01:32:19 --> 01:32:23
historical View and the social sciences

01:32:21 --> 01:32:25
has always been that judgments are based

01:32:22 --> 01:32:26
solely on content information with

01:32:24 --> 01:32:27
individuals being assumed to form

01:32:25 --> 01:32:29
judgments by systematically evaluating

01:32:26 --> 01:32:31
all available content information in an

01:32:28 --> 01:32:33
unbiased manner oh my God however over

01:32:30 --> 01:32:34
the past three decades a considerable

01:32:32 --> 01:32:36
amount of research has challenged this

01:32:33 --> 01:32:37
assumption by showing that judgments may

01:32:35 --> 01:32:39
be formed not only on the basis of

01:32:36 --> 01:32:40
content information cognitive judgment

01:32:38 --> 01:32:42
but also on the basis of feelings

01:32:39 --> 01:32:44
affective judgment it is now well

01:32:41 --> 01:32:46
accepted that judgment can be both

01:32:43 --> 01:32:48
effective and cognitive and here's where

01:32:45 --> 01:32:49
the good stuff comes whether it is one

01:32:47 --> 01:32:51
or the other depends on a multitude of

01:32:48 --> 01:32:52
actors number one the salience of the

01:32:50 --> 01:32:54
ffective feelings number two the

01:32:51 --> 01:32:55
representativeness of the effective

01:32:53 --> 01:32:56
feelings for the target number three the

01:32:54 --> 01:32:58
relevance of the feelings to the

01:32:55 --> 01:33:00
Judgment number four the evaluative

01:32:57 --> 01:33:02
malleability of the judgment and number

01:32:59 --> 01:33:04
five the level of processing intensity

01:33:01 --> 01:33:06
and here is the ultimate Clincher for

01:33:03 --> 01:33:08
this I will discuss these in turn and

01:33:05 --> 01:33:09
ultimately argue that that generally

01:33:07 --> 01:33:11
speaking in day-to-day life the

01:33:08 --> 01:33:13
circumstances are generally those that

01:33:10 --> 01:33:14
result in affective rather than

01:33:12 --> 01:33:17
cognitive judgments and decision making

01:33:13 --> 01:33:19
so if we can disentangle all the

01:33:16 --> 01:33:22
verbosity from that paragraph what I've

01:33:18 --> 01:33:23
done is I've laid out the five main bits

01:33:21 --> 01:33:25
of the essay in terms of structure and

01:33:22 --> 01:33:26
I've used numbered points for that

01:33:24 --> 01:33:28
rather than just a list because numbered

01:33:25 --> 01:33:29
makes it really really obvious to The

01:33:27 --> 01:33:32
Examiner that I've got a good structure

01:33:28 --> 01:33:33
I've also said exactly what the answer

01:33:31 --> 01:33:35
to the question is the question is

01:33:32 --> 01:33:36
asking whether our judgments are

01:33:34 --> 01:33:38
cognitive or illogical or effective

01:33:35 --> 01:33:40
motional and instead of wishing washing

01:33:37 --> 01:33:42
around it I have said in this essay I

01:33:39 --> 01:33:44
will argue that they are emotional

01:33:41 --> 01:33:45
rather than cognitive in most elements

01:33:43 --> 01:33:47
of day-to-day life so I'm telling the

01:33:44 --> 01:33:48
xaminer look I'm answering the question

01:33:46 --> 01:33:50
this is what you're going to get from me

01:33:47 --> 01:33:51
and finally I've added a little bit of

01:33:49 --> 01:33:53
lair hopefully with this stuff about

01:33:50 --> 01:33:55
he historical context I probably got

01:33:52 --> 01:33:56
hat from a textbook from a review paper

01:33:54 --> 01:33:57
somewhere and I've probably phrased into

01:33:55 --> 01:33:59
my own notes and obviously this is just

01:33:56 --> 01:34:00
my plan so in the exam I won't quite be

01:33:58 --> 01:34:03
using it word for word so it's

01:34:00 --> 01:34:06
absolutely not plagiarism it's using you

01:34:02 --> 01:34:07
know useful resources to create a bit of

01:34:05 --> 01:34:08
lair by adding a bit of historical

01:34:06 --> 01:34:10
context so hopefully this introduction

01:34:07 --> 01:34:12
covers all three points structure

01:34:09 --> 01:34:13
answering the question and a bit of

01:34:11 --> 01:34:15
lair now I'm going to leave it at that

video description and then a card up

01:45:09 --> 01:45:13
there somewhere where I go through the

01:45:11 --> 01:45:15
vidence in much more detail but

01:45:12 --> 01:45:17
essentially what active recall means is

01:45:14 --> 01:45:19
testing yourself and the reason

01:45:16 --> 01:45:21
testing ourselves is so amazingly

01:45:18 --> 01:45:24
valuable is because the way the brain

01:45:21 --> 01:45:26
works it's all based around how many

01:45:23 --> 01:45:29
times and how

01:45:25 --> 01:45:30
much you retrieve information from

01:45:28 --> 01:45:33
your brain so we all have this

01:45:29 --> 01:45:35
misconception that in order to study we

01:45:32 --> 01:45:36
have to put stuff into our brains but

01:45:34 --> 01:45:38
actually it's it's flipped on its head

01:45:35 --> 01:45:40
if you look at the evidence the the

01:45:37 --> 01:45:42
actual way to remember anything and to

01:45:39 --> 01:45:44
make anything stick is by retrieving

01:45:41 --> 01:45:46
information from our brains rather than

01:45:43 --> 01:45:47
trying to put it back in so let's say we

01:45:45 --> 01:45:50
read something once and we've understood

01:45:46 --> 01:45:51
a topic at that point the most effective

01:45:49 --> 01:45:53
thing we can do with our time is ask

01:45:50 --> 01:45:54
ourselves questions about that topic

01:45:52 --> 01:45:56
yeah that's basically how the method

01:45:54 --> 01:45:58
works I'll stop droning on about this

01:45:55 --> 01:45:59
now let's now talk about the method in a

01:45:57 --> 01:46:01
bit more detail and I'll show you how

01:45:58 --> 01:46:02
you can use various different apps to

01:46:00 --> 01:46:03
achieve this same effect all right so

01:46:01 --> 01:46:05
let's talk about this method in a bit

01:46:03 --> 01:46:06
more detail and one of the common

01:46:04 --> 01:46:08
questions about this method is you know

01:46:05 --> 01:46:09
hat do you do if you don't know the

01:46:07 --> 01:46:11
answer to one of the questions that

01:46:08 --> 01:46:12
you've written so at the start we've

01:46:10 --> 01:46:14
gone through our lectures and we've just

01:46:11 --> 01:46:16
converted everything into just questions

01:46:13 --> 01:46:17
we've gone through our textbook and

01:46:15 --> 01:46:18
we've just written questions for

01:46:16 --> 01:46:20
ourselves but then when we're going

01:46:17 --> 01:46:21
through the questions obviously there's

01:46:19 --> 01:46:24
going to be stuff that we don't remember

01:46:20 --> 01:46:26
the answer to so for example if I read

01:46:23 --> 01:46:27
what is the capacitance of biomembranes

01:46:25 --> 01:46:29
you know

01:46:26 --> 01:46:31
I might not remember that from the

01:46:28 --> 01:46:32
lecture notes but the key thing of the

01:46:30 --> 01:46:34
Sage's method is that he never writes

01:46:31 --> 01:46:36
down the answer to these questions he

01:46:33 --> 01:46:37
trusts that he'll be able to find the

01:46:35 --> 01:46:39
information in the lecture notes or in

01:46:36 --> 01:46:41
the textbook or on Google and therefore

01:46:38 --> 01:46:42
he doesn't have to waste the time to

01:46:40 --> 01:46:43
actually write down the answer for all

01:46:41 --> 01:46:45
of these and that's obviously a benefit

01:46:42 --> 01:46:47
because it means we save time and it's a

01:46:44 --> 01:46:48
bit more efficient but obviously the

01:46:46 --> 01:46:50
drawback is that then when we're going

01:46:47 --> 01:46:52
through stuff it does take a little bit

01:46:49 --> 01:46:54
of time to then find that information in

01:46:51 --> 01:46:56
the right place but this isn't

01:46:53 --> 01:46:57
necessarily a bad thing it's not

01:46:55 --> 01:46:58
necessarily a bad thing that in order to

01:46:56 --> 01:47:00
get information we have to go back to

01:46:57 --> 01:47:02
election notes because at least for us

01:46:59 --> 01:47:03
in Cambridge the lecture notes are

01:47:01 --> 01:47:06
usually quite well structured and quite

01:47:02 --> 01:47:07
well organized and so the act of finding

01:47:05 --> 01:47:09
information in election notes means that

01:47:06 --> 01:47:11
we'll be like we'll we'll be able to see

01:47:08 --> 01:47:13
what section the information's in be

01:47:10 --> 01:47:15
like okay that's the capacitance of

01:47:12 --> 01:47:18
biomembranes and we'll see where it fits

01:47:14 --> 01:47:19
into the bigger picture equally if we're

01:47:17 --> 01:47:20
having to go into a textbook textbooks

01:47:18 --> 01:47:22
are usually very well written because

01:47:19 --> 01:47:23
you know they've been around for years

01:47:21 --> 01:47:24
and they're written by clever people and

01:47:22 --> 01:47:25
stuff

01:47:23 --> 01:47:27
um and so if we're finding information

01:47:24 --> 01:47:29
in a textbook again we're going to be

01:47:26 --> 01:47:30
getting that info information in context

01:47:28 --> 01:47:32
in the context of the rest of the

01:47:29 --> 01:47:33
subject and then maybe instead of just

01:47:31 --> 01:47:35
reading about the capacitance of

01:47:32 --> 01:47:37
biomembranes we'll read a little bit

01:47:34 --> 01:47:39
more about biomebranes and kind of just

01:47:36 --> 01:47:40
understand the subject a little bit

01:47:38 --> 01:47:42
better and so what state is doing is

01:47:39 --> 01:47:43
like number one the first pass through

01:47:41 --> 01:47:45
the lecture he's just converting into

01:47:42 --> 01:47:46
questions and then the second time he

01:47:44 --> 01:47:48
comes around to revise the subject he's

01:47:45 --> 01:47:50
going to go through the questions ask

01:47:47 --> 01:47:52
himself one by one and then what he does

01:47:49 --> 01:47:54
is that he's color coding

01:47:51 --> 01:47:55
um in red for example over here the

01:47:53 --> 01:47:57
questions that he didn't know the answer

01:47:54 --> 01:47:59
to so that the next time around if he's

01:47:56 --> 01:48:00
hort on time he won't go through every

01:47:58 --> 01:48:02
single question he'll just go through

01:47:59 --> 01:48:04
the questions that he's marked read and

01:48:01 --> 01:48:05
then let's say the third time around if

01:48:03 --> 01:48:06
there are questions that he didn't get

01:48:04 --> 01:48:07
in the second time he'll mark them as

01:48:05 --> 01:48:09
blue and then the third time around

01:48:06 --> 01:48:11
he'll just go over the blue questions so

01:48:08 --> 01:48:13
this becomes a very efficient way of

01:48:10 --> 01:48:15
only doing the questions that we know we

01:48:12 --> 01:48:16
got wrong previously and obviously

01:48:14 --> 01:48:17
before the exam and at some point we

01:48:15 --> 01:48:18
want to be going through every single

01:48:16 --> 01:48:20
question because one of the other

01:48:18 --> 01:48:22
Concepts in effective studying is called

01:48:19 --> 01:48:24
spaced repetition and again I'll link my

01:48:21 --> 01:48:25
video about that down here and then a

01:48:23 --> 01:48:27
card up there and you can check it out

01:48:24 --> 01:48:30
more but basically the idea is that

01:48:26 --> 01:48:31
crumming isn't effective it obviously

01:48:29 --> 01:48:33
works in the short term we've all done

01:48:30 --> 01:48:34
cramming for exams but actually if we

01:48:32 --> 01:48:36
want information to stick over a very

01:48:33 --> 01:48:39
long term period we want to be repeating

01:48:36 --> 01:48:41
that information at spaced intervals to

01:48:38 --> 01:48:43
vercome What's called the forgetting

01:48:40 --> 01:48:44
effect or the forgetting curve that was

01:48:42 --> 01:48:46
discovered by a guy called ebbing house

01:48:43 --> 01:48:48
in like the 1800s I think but yeah more

01:48:45 --> 01:48:50
information in my space repetition video

01:48:47 --> 01:48:52
basically it's all about active recall

01:48:49 --> 01:48:54
and space repetition and this method

01:48:51 --> 01:48:55
really works in that sense and we can

01:48:53 --> 01:48:57
see here he's even color coded some of

01:48:54 --> 01:48:58
them in purple and I suspect those are

01:48:56 --> 01:49:00
the questions that he was a little bit

01:48:57 --> 01:49:03
struggling with on his fourth pass of

01:48:59 --> 01:49:04
doing these doing these questions this

01:49:02 --> 01:49:05
n't the sort of method that you can

01:49:03 --> 01:49:07
just kind of do once and then forget

01:49:04 --> 01:49:08
about and then be like oh well I haven't

01:49:06 --> 01:49:09
done any work for a year therefore I'm

01:49:07 --> 01:49:12
going to cram this in two days before

01:49:09 --> 01:49:14
the exam you probably could uh but it

01:49:11 --> 01:49:15
would be cramming it wouldn't be

01:49:13 --> 01:49:17
long-term knowledge that would stick

01:49:14 --> 01:49:19
whereas doing this and applying space

01:49:16 --> 01:49:21
repetition to it would really be magical

01:49:18 --> 01:49:23
and I'll just show you how I would apply

01:49:20 --> 01:49:24
this method personally so um I've you

01:49:22 --> 01:49:26
know active recall is the best thing

01:49:23 --> 01:49:28
ever so I've been using variants of this

01:49:25 --> 01:49:30
method for a few years is now I just

01:49:27 --> 01:49:32
haven't used them as well as I say it

01:49:29 --> 01:49:35
has because I feel you know I get lazy

01:49:31 --> 01:49:37
and I'm not great but he is really good

01:49:34 --> 01:49:39
at kind of actually doing the work

01:49:36 --> 01:49:40
anyway the other day I Was preparing for

01:49:38 --> 01:49:42
a supervision where I'm I'm teaching

a link to a few of my videos on that if

01:49:52 --> 01:49:56
you if you care but I think notion is

01:49:57 --> 01:50:02
is I can for example if I go into fetal

does the fetal circulation need to be

special what does the oxygen

he moment I'm not really in the market

01:50:22 --> 01:50:27
as well again through a toggle box so

01:50:25 --> 01:50:28
actually I haven't written down the

01:50:26 --> 01:50:30

01:50:27 --> 01:50:32
weird but like what I do is that for for

questions that I know I know the answer

01:50:31 --> 01:50:36
to or that I know I can figure out the

answer to or I know I can find the

01:50:39 --> 01:50:43
does the fetal circulation need to be

01:50:40 --> 01:50:45
special clearly that's because we need

01:50:42 --> 01:50:47
to overcome the problem that when you're

a fetus I.E inside your mum uh you don't

need to get your oxygen from the

mother's placental blood flow and all

that stuff therefore you need to be

01:50:51 --> 01:50:55

01:51:00 --> 01:51:05
don't need to waste the time writing the

ever get to the point where I read that

he answer is then I will go back to the

fficient in minimizing the amount of

information I'm having to put into my

notes because as we've said and as all

the evidence shows summarizing

01:51:16 --> 01:51:21
information taking notes with the book

01:51:18 --> 01:51:22
open is just not a very effective way of

01:51:20 --> 01:51:24
studying

01:51:21 --> 01:51:26
anyway this one what is the oxygen

like comparing maternal versus fetal I

know what it looks like in my head and

otion and what I'm doing is that for a

shortcut on the Mac to select a

like and then I can untoggle this and

move on what are the various adaptations

of the fetal circulation

so number one two and three so these are

stuff that I wrote down because in the

lecture notes this was in a few

01:52:30 --> 01:52:33
different sections and so I just thought

01:52:31 --> 01:52:36
you know what I'm gonna take 10 seconds

01:52:32 --> 01:52:38
just type out what these answers are but

01:52:35 --> 01:52:40
for example if I looked at fetal Hemi

01:52:37 --> 01:52:42
hemoglobin relinquishes that oxygen at

01:52:39 --> 01:52:44
lower oxygen tensions if that sentence

01:52:41 --> 01:52:46
didn't make sense to me I wouldn't just

01:52:43 --> 01:52:47
ake it at face value I would obviously

01:52:45 --> 01:52:49
go back to the lecture notes or go back

01:52:46 --> 01:52:51
to my textbook and figure out what I'm

01:52:48 --> 01:52:53
struggling with why this sentence

01:52:50 --> 01:52:55
doesn't make sense to me and work out

01:52:52 --> 01:52:58
what like to try and understand what's

01:52:54 --> 01:53:00
going on and this all comes back to like

01:52:57 --> 01:53:01
it's all very well at doing active

01:52:59 --> 01:53:02
recall it's all very well doing space

01:53:00 --> 01:53:04
repetition and doing all of this stuff

01:53:01 --> 01:53:06
but really the thing that trumps all of

01:53:03 --> 01:53:08
these things is to understand the

01:53:05 --> 01:53:09
content there is literally no point in

01:53:07 --> 01:53:11
just memorizing facts if we don't

01:53:08 --> 01:53:13
understand the broad principles that

01:53:10 --> 01:53:15
underlie them and so as I'm going

01:53:12 --> 01:53:16
through these questions as any of my

01:53:14 --> 01:53:18
friends who do effective studying as

01:53:15 --> 01:53:19
we're going through our questions we're

01:53:17 --> 01:53:21
trying to understand the topic and if we

01:53:18 --> 01:53:23
ver get to a point where we're like oh

01:53:20 --> 01:53:25
I'm not quite sure what that means we

01:53:22 --> 01:53:26
will take the time to look it up because

01:53:24 --> 01:53:28
specially with effective learning like

01:53:25 --> 01:53:30
this is again there's this misconception

01:53:27 --> 01:53:32
that learning should be easy that you

01:53:29 --> 01:53:33
know the best way to learn is by finding

01:53:31 --> 01:53:34
a really condensed set of notes and just

01:53:32 --> 01:53:37
reading it and getting that information

01:53:33 --> 01:53:38
into our heads but again it's sort of

01:53:36 --> 01:53:41
the other way around it's the more

01:53:37 --> 01:53:42
ffortful the more hard it feels to be

01:53:40 --> 01:53:43
learning something the more we're

01:53:41 --> 01:53:45
actually going to be learning it and

01:53:42 --> 01:53:46
there's again so much evidence where

01:53:44 --> 01:53:48
people have have done studies on

01:53:45 --> 01:53:50
students where they rate how hard they

01:53:47 --> 01:53:52
found the subject and how hard it was to

01:53:49 --> 01:53:54
learn about it and you find that those

01:53:51 --> 01:53:56
students perform better on the exam and

01:53:53 --> 01:53:57
learn more consistently and and have

01:53:55 --> 01:53:59
that knowledge for a longer period of

01:53:56 --> 01:54:00
time because as we're grappling with

01:53:58 --> 01:54:02
stuff as we're putting effort into

01:54:00 --> 01:54:04
learning our brain is forming these

01:54:01 --> 01:54:06
connections and the way I think of it is

01:54:03 --> 01:54:07
ort of like going to the gym and it's

01:54:05 --> 01:54:10
like you know if I can bench press 80

01:54:06 --> 01:54:12
kilograms I can't I wish I can probably

01:54:09 --> 01:54:15
bench press about 70 kilograms but you

01:54:11 --> 01:54:17
know when I bench 70 kilograms that is

01:54:14 --> 01:54:18
effortful that is when my muscles are

01:54:16 --> 01:54:19
going to grow whereas when I bench 20

01:54:17 --> 01:54:21
kilograms then you know basically

01:54:18 --> 01:54:23
nothing's going to happen because it's

01:54:20 --> 01:54:24
easy so it's kind of the same with

01:54:22 --> 01:54:26
studying like when it's difficult we

01:54:24 --> 01:54:27
your actively forming Connections in our

01:54:25 --> 01:54:29
brain and then we sleep and then those

01:54:26 --> 01:54:31
color connections get solidified when it

01:54:28 --> 01:54:32
feels easy when we're just like oh yeah

01:54:30 --> 01:54:34
reading like reading and highlighting

01:54:31 --> 01:54:35
and making notes is really easy that's

01:54:33 --> 01:54:36
why we love doing it because it feels

01:54:34 --> 01:54:38
really productive we kind of go through

01:54:35 --> 01:54:40
pretty colors and we write down stuff

01:54:37 --> 01:54:42
but we're not actually using our brains

01:54:39 --> 01:54:43
and the more we use our brains the more

01:54:41 --> 01:54:46
ffort for learning is the more its

01:54:42 --> 01:54:48
information is going to stick anyway

um next one what are fetal shunts and

what do they do again I kind of wrote

about this it's sort of a balance of

compression versus context textbooks and

hey're very long very broad but they

give you the context they help

01:55:19 --> 01:55:24
you understand the topic whereas when we

take notes we have compressed all that

information down

01:55:24 --> 01:55:29
and so when I see rated LA because Visa

lungs don't work I know immediately

01:55:28 --> 01:55:32
exactly what that means I could explain

01:55:29 --> 01:55:34
it to a five-year-old if I wanted to but

01:55:31 --> 01:55:35
if a first-year medic who had never done
